> **Tags:** #fluency-illusion, #metacognitive-calibration, #miscalibration

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[formative-assessment-focused-analysis-2026-03-24]])*
> **Q:** Seed 3
> **A:** **Q:** What is a calibration event, and what distinguishes it from ordinary information delivery?
> **A:** A calibration event is an assessment activity that produces an internally-generated encounter with the discrepancy between felt-knowing and actual-knowing. It is distinguished by being internally generated (cannot be attributed to external factors) and by producing a prediction error the monitoring system cannot rationalize away. Retrieval failure is a paradigm case: the student's monitoring system predicted "I know this" and the retrieval attempt disconfirmed it unambiguously.
> **Source:** Phase IV, calibration event taxonomy; Appendix Lexicon
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Type:** Distinction
> **Tags:** #calibration-event, #testing-effect, #retrieval-practice

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[formative-assessment-focused-analysis-2026-03-24]])*
> **Q:** Seed 4
> **A:** **Q:** Why does the Hattie-Timperley finding that self-regulation-level feedback outperforms task-level feedback support the MCM over the ITM?
> **A:** The ITM predicts that feedback content quality is the primary variable; it cannot explain why feedback that builds monitoring infrastructure (self-regulation level) produces larger gains than feedback that delivers better information (task level). The MCM predicts exactly this: gains come from building the monitoring system's accuracy, not from maximizing information delivered to a monitoring system that may not be accurately tracking the gap.
> **Source:** Phase III, Hattie & Timperley evidence review
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Type:** Connection
> **Tags:** #hattie-timperley, #feedback-levels, #monitoring-infrastructure

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[formative-assessment-focused-analysis-2026-03-24]])*
> **Q:** Seed 5
> **A:** **Q:** What is the Calibration-Integrated Model's key departure from both the ITM and the simple MCM?
> **A:** It proposes a multiplicative relationship between information quality and calibration receptivity: Effectiveness = f(Information Quality × Calibration Receptivity). This is a departure from both additive accounts (ITM adds information; MCM adds calibration separately) because it predicts that even high-quality information multiplied by near-zero calibration receptivity yields near-zero gain. The multiplicative structure explains why information-rich, gain-poor patterns occur.
> **Source:** Phase VI, original synthesis
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Type:** Definition
> **Tags:** #calibration-integrated-model, #original-synthesis, #multiplicative-relationship

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[formative-assessment-focused-analysis-2026-03-24]])*
> **Q:** Seed 6
> **A:** **Q:** What does the Nelson-Narens monitoring-control framework contribute to understanding why formative assessment works?
> **A:** It provides the architectural account of how external feedback can fail to influence behavior even when it is accurate. The meta level monitors the object level and regulates it based on monitoring signals. If the meta level's monitoring signals are inaccurate (miscalibrated), control will be miscalibrated even if external information is provided, because the meta level filters external information through its current monitoring model.
> **Source:** Phase II, Nelson-Narens framework; Phase IV, monitoring-control loop
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Type:** Process
> **Tags:** #nelson-narens, #monitoring-control, #metacognitive-architecture

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[formative-assessment-focused-analysis-2026-03-24]])*
> **Q:** Seed 7
> **A:** **Q:** What is the Calibration-First design principle, and why does it reverse common practice?
> **A:** Calibrate First, Inform Second: generate a calibration event (retrieval attempt, prediction, peer explanation) *before* presenting correct answers or elaborated feedback. This reverses common practice, which delivers information and trusts the monitoring system to register it. The reversal is justified by the MCM: information can only do its work when the monitoring system is accurately tracking the gap the information addresses; a calibration event is what repairs monitoring accuracy.
> **Source:** Phase V, design implications; Appendix Protocol
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Type:** Application
> **Tags:** #calibration-first, #assessment-design, #instructional-design

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[formative-assessment-focused-analysis-2026-03-24]])*
> **Q:** Seed 8
> **A:** **Q:** Why does the Scaffolded-Recalibration Gradient predict that maintaining formative assessment scaffolds indefinitely is counterproductive?
> **A:** If external calibration (formative tests, teacher feedback) is always available, students may develop accurate monitoring only when external support is present, without developing autonomous internal monitoring infrastructure. The gradient prescribes fading external calibration tools as monitoring accuracy improves, shifting responsibility to self-testing and self-prediction — otherwise scaffold-dependence replaces the autonomous self-regulation that formative assessment was meant to build.
> **Source:** Phase IV, scaffolding dimension; Phase VII, transfer to athletic coaching
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Type:** Process
> **Tags:** #scaffolded-fading, #scaffold-dependence, #self-regulated-learning

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Flashcard 1 — Definition
> **A:** **Q:** How did Flavell (1976) define metacognition, and what are its two major components as distinguished by Brown (1987)?
> **A:** Flavell defined metacognition as "cognition about cognition" — thinking about one's own thinking processes. Brown distinguished two components: *knowledge of cognition* (awareness of cognitive processes, strategies, and states — including declarative, procedural, and conditional knowledge) and *regulation of cognition* (executive control including planning, monitoring, and evaluating).
> **Source:** Section I
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #metacognition, #definition, #foundational

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Flashcard 2 — Distinction
> **A:** **Q:** What is the difference between a judgment of learning (JOL) and a feeling of knowing (FOK)?
> **A:** JOLs occur *during or after study* and estimate how well material has been encoded ("How well have I learned this?"). FOKs occur *during failed retrieval* and estimate the likelihood of future recognition ("I can't recall this now, but I think I would recognise it"). JOLs assess encoding quality; FOKs assess the residual trace of information that cannot be currently retrieved.
> **Source:** Section III
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #metacognitive-monitoring, #distinction

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Flashcard 3 — Process
> **A:** **Q:** Describe the three phases of Zimmerman's self-regulated learning cycle and their PKM workflow equivalents.
> **A:** (1) *Forethought phase* (goal-setting, strategic planning) → PKM capture and planning workflows. (2) *Performance phase* (strategy implementation, self-monitoring) → Active note-making with elaboration and connection. (3) *Self-reflection phase* (self-judgment, self-reaction) → Review, evaluation, and system modification.
> **Source:** Section IV
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #self-regulated-learning, #pkm, #process

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Flashcard 4 — Connection
> **A:** **Q:** How does the fluency illusion undermine metacognitive calibration in the context of PKM practice?
> **A:** The fluency illusion causes practitioners to mistake *familiarity with material* for *understanding of material*. In PKM, re-reading one's own notes produces high processing fluency (the text is familiar), which the monitoring system misinterprets as evidence of comprehension. This inflates confidence without improving actual knowledge, worsening calibration. The antidote is retrieval-based review: attempting to recall content before reading it.
> **Source:** Sections III, V
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #fluency-illusion, #calibration, #pkm

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Flashcard 5 — Application
> **A:** **Q:** What are the three tiers of the Extended Metacognitive Architecture Model (EMAM)?
> **A:** *Tier 1: Internal metacognition* — classical neural monitoring and regulation (flexible but noisy). *Tier 2: Scaffolded metacognition* — external systems that support internal processes (templates, visualisations, schedules; reduces bias but requires active engagement). *Tier 3: Extended metacognition* — external systems that *constitute* metacognitive processes through functional integration (algorithms that perform monitoring/control autonomously; most reliable but risks atrophy).
> **Source:** Section VI
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #emam, #extended-metacognition, #framework

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Flashcard 6 — Distinction
> **A:** **Q:** What is the key difference between scaffolded metacognition (Tier 2) and extended metacognition (Tier 3) in the EMAM?
> **A:** In *scaffolded metacognition*, the cognitive agent remains the locus of metacognitive processing — the external system provides input and structure, but the human does the monitoring/regulation work. In *extended metacognition*, the external system *performs* the metacognitive functions autonomously through functional integration — the processing is genuinely distributed between internal and external components. The distinction turns on whether the human or the system is doing the metacognitive work.
> **Source:** Section VI
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #emam, #distinction, #extended-mind

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Flashcard 7 — Connection
> **A:** **Q:** What is the monitoring-control coupling, and why does it matter for PKM design?
> **A:** The monitoring-control coupling (Nelson & Narens) is the degree to which monitoring outputs are effectively translated into regulatory control actions. High coupling: problem detected → corrective action taken. Low coupling: problem detected → no effective response. PKM design can tighten this coupling by embedding regulatory responses in monitoring mechanisms (e.g., spaced repetition adjusting review difficulty automatically) and make it visible through logging both monitoring events and regulatory responses.
> **Source:** Section IV
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #monitoring-control-coupling, #pkm-design

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Flashcard 8 — Process
> **A:** **Q:** List the four phases of the Metacognitive PKM Lifecycle and the dominant metacognitive characteristic of each.
> **A:** (1) *Unconscious Capture* — metacognitive monitoring dominated by fluency illusions; regulation ad hoc. (2) *Structured Processing* — external scaffolding provides regulatory structure; monitoring improves through retrieval practice. (3) *Reflective Integration* — practitioner monitors and regulates *system use* itself; calibration improves. (4) *Metacognitive Mastery* — flexible, context-sensitive regulation; conscious management of the internal-external metacognition balance.
> **Source:** Section VII
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #lifecycle, #development, #pkm

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Flashcard 9 — Definition
> **A:** **Q:** What is cognitive offloading, and what is the "PKM calibration paradox" it creates?
> **A:** Cognitive offloading is using external resources to reduce internal cognitive demands. The PKM calibration paradox: creating comprehensive notes can *worsen* calibration because the practitioner mistakes *access fluency* (knowing where to find information in their system) for *retrieval fluency* (actually knowing the information). The notes serve as external memory that the practitioner mistakenly attributes to their internal memory, inflating confidence.
> **Source:** Section V
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #cognitive-offloading, #calibration, #paradox

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Flashcard 10 — Connection
> **A:** **Q:** How does the parity principle from the extended mind thesis apply to metacognitive tools?
> **A:** The parity principle states: if an external process functions in a way that, were it done internally, we would count it as cognitive, then it IS cognitive regardless of physical location. Applied to metacognition: if a PKM system's spaced repetition algorithm performs the same function as an internal FOK judgment (assessing whether knowledge is sufficiently consolidated for retrieval), the parity principle suggests we should count it as a metacognitive process — making the system part of the agent's metacognitive architecture.
> **Source:** Section VI
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #parity-principle, #extended-mind, #metacognition

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-and-reading-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 1 — Definition
> **A:** **Q:** What is comprehension monitoring, and at what levels does it operate?
> **A:** The continuous metacognitive evaluation of text representation adequacy against multiple standards (lexical, propositional, coherence, situation-model). Failures at any level constitute candidate triggers for regulatory response. Most poor comprehenders monitor at lexical/surface levels but neglect coherence and situation-model levels.
> **Source:** Phase II, Section 2.1; Phase IV, Section 4.1
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #metacognition, #reading, #comprehension-monitoring

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-and-reading-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 2 — Distinction
> **A:** **Q:** What is the difference between the textbase and the situation model, and why does the distinction matter for reading comprehension?
> **A:** The textbase is a propositional representation of what the text explicitly states; the situation model is the reader's integrated mental representation of the state of affairs described, enriched by inference and prior knowledge. Only situation-model construction constitutes deep comprehension; textbase representation supports only surface paraphrasing.
> **Source:** Phase II, Section 2.2
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #reading, #situation-model, #textbase

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-and-reading-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 3 — Definition
> **A:** **Q:** What is the fluency illusion and what cognitive mechanism produces it?
> **A:** The misattribution of surface-reading fluency (decoding ease, lexical retrieval speed) to comprehension depth. Produced by the monitoring system's reliance on processing ease as a comprehension signal — but fluency carries information about surface-level processing, not situation-model construction. Cause: monitoring operates at the wrong level of text representation.
> **Source:** Phase II, Section 2.2
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #metacognition, #fluency-illusion, #monitoring-failure

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-and-reading-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 4 — Process
> **A:** **Q:** Describe the Nelson-Narens monitoring-control architecture as applied to reading.
> **A:** Object level = direct text processing (decoding, parsing, semantic integration). Meta level = metacognitive monitoring of object-level processes. Monitoring flows upward: represents state of comprehension. Control flows downward: adjusts object-level strategy in response. Effective comprehension monitoring requires: (1) accurate monitoring signals, (2) reliable monitoring-control coupling, (3) appropriate control responses.
> **Source:** Phase II, Section 2.3
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #nelson-narens, #metacognitive-monitoring, #reading

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-and-reading-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 5 — Distinction
> **A:** **Q:** What is the difference between metacognitive monitoring failure and motivational disengagement in the context of reading comprehension deficits?
> **A:** Monitoring failure = the reader genuinely does not detect comprehension breakdown (cognitive deficit in the detection mechanism). Motivational disengagement = the reader detects failure but suppresses regulatory response (motivationally driven suppression, often to protect self-efficacy). These two pathways require different instructional responses.
> **Source:** Phase VII, Section 3 (Tensions)
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #metacognition, #motivation, #comprehension-monitoring

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-and-reading-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 6 — Definition
> **A:** **Q:** What are declarative, procedural, and conditional metacognitive knowledge, and how do they apply to reading strategy instruction?
> **A:** Declarative = knowing *that* (strategy names, self-knowledge); Procedural = knowing *how* (strategy execution); Conditional = knowing *when and why* (appropriateness conditions). Reading strategy instruction most commonly develops only declarative and procedural knowledge; conditional knowledge — the most critical for transfer — is most commonly neglected.
> **Source:** Phase III, Section 3.3
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #metacognitive-knowledge, #strategy-instruction, #reading

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-and-reading-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 7 — Application
> **A:** **Q:** What are the four strategies in reciprocal teaching and what metacognitive function does each perform?
> **A:** (1) Predicting — activates prior knowledge, establishes comprehension goals; (2) Questioning — enforces self-monitoring via explicit comprehension question generation; (3) Clarifying — comprehension monitoring and fix-up strategy deployment for detected failures; (4) Summarizing — coherence evaluation at the discourse level, tests situation model construction.
> **Source:** Phase V, Section 5.1
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #reciprocal-teaching, #metacognitive-strategies, #reading

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-and-reading-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 8 — Connection
> **A:** **Q:** How does Verbose Efficiency Theory connect decoding fluency to reading comprehension via the working memory mechanism?
> **A:** Inefficient decoding (non-automatic lexical access) consumes working memory resources with lexical-phonological retrieval, leaving insufficient capacity for comprehension processes (coherence monitoring, inference generation, situation-model construction). Therefore, reading fluency is a necessary (though not sufficient) condition for metacognitive comprehension monitoring in demanding texts.
> **Source:** Phase IV, Section 4.5
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #verbal-efficiency-theory, #working-memory, #reading-fluency

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-and-reading-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 9 — Distinction
> **A:** **Q:** What distinguishes effective annotation strategies from passive highlighting in terms of metacognitive engagement?
> **A:** Passive highlighting = topographic marking of verbatim text; associated with fluency illusion rather than comprehension monitoring. Effective annotation = content-transforming marks (questions, summaries, connections, evaluations) that require comprehension evaluation, inference generation, and prior-knowledge integration. Effective annotation externalizes the metacognitive regulatory dialogue; passive highlighting does not.
> **Source:** Phase V, Section 5.4
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #annotation, #metacognition, #reading-strategies

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-and-reading-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 10 — Process
> **A:** **Q:** What are the four stages of effective fix-up strategy deployment during reading?
> **A:** (1) Signal detection — monitoring detects comprehension failure; (2) Failure diagnosis — reader identifies the level/type of failure (lexical, propositional, coherence, situation-model); (3) Strategy selection — conditional metacognitive knowledge guides response selection; (4) Strategy execution and evaluation — strategy deployed and monitoring re-evaluates whether failure is resolved.
> **Source:** Phase IV, Section 4.2
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #fix-up-strategy, #comprehension-monitoring, #metacognition

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-and-reading-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 11 — Connection
> **A:** **Q:** How does the Matthew Effect in reading relate to metacognitive monitoring capacity?
> **A:** Poor readers have reduced metacognitive monitoring capacity because demanding decoding exhausts working memory; reduced monitoring leads to reduced comprehension; reduced comprehension means less schema building and vocabulary growth, which keeps decoding demanding and keeps monitoring capacity suppressed. The failure cycle is: poor decoding → high cognitive load → suppressed monitoring → comprehension failure → reduced vocabulary and schema growth → continued poor decoding.
> **Source:** Phase IV, Section 4.5
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #matthew-effect, #metacognition, #reading, #cognitive-load

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-and-reading-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 12 — Definition
> **A:** **Q:** What does "self-regulated reading" mean and how does it relate to Zimmerman's cyclical SRL model?
> **A:** Self-regulated reading is metacognitive, goal-directed, and strategically enacted text comprehension. Maps onto Zimmerman's three phases: (1) Forethought = goal-setting, prior knowledge activation, strategy planning; (2) Performance = comprehension monitoring, strategy deployment, attention regulation; (3) Self-reflection = outcome evaluation, attribution, strategy adaptation. Distinguishes reading as self-directed enterprise from externally regulated strategy use.
> **Source:** Phase III, Section 3.4; Phase II, Section 2.1
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #self-regulated-learning, #reading, #metacognition

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-and-reading-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 13 — Application
> **A:** **Q:** What is the KWL strategy, and which metacognitive reading functions does each component serve?
> **A:** K (What do I *Know*?) = prior knowledge activation and establishing comprehension baseline; W (What do I *Want* to know?) = goal-setting and directing monitoring attention; L (What have I *Learned*?) = comprehension evaluation, gap detection, and situation model assessment. Collectively: the KWL sequence operationalizes the forethought-monitoring-self-reflection SRL cycle for a single reading session.
> **Source:** Phase V, Section 5.3
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #KWL-strategy, #metacognition, #reading

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-and-reading-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 14 — Connection
> **A:** **Q:** How does the elaborative interrogation strategy operationalize the depth-of-processing principle in reading?
> **A:** Depth-of-processing principle: semantic/elaborative processing produces more durable encoding than surface processing. Elaborative interrogation operationalizes deep processing by requiring the reader to generate *why/how* explanations for text propositions, thereby enforcing semantic elaboration and prior-knowledge integration. The strategy converts passive proposition reception into active inferential construction — the functional equivalent of forced deep processing.
> **Source:** Phase III, Section 3.5; Phase VII, Lexicon
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #elaborative-interrogation, #levels-of-processing, #reading

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-skills-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 1 — Definition: Metacognition
> **A:** **Q:** What is Flavell's definition of metacognition, and what two broad categories does it comprise?
> **A:** Metacognition is cognition about cognition — knowledge and processes that take one's own cognitive states and products as their objects. It comprises (a) metacognitive knowledge (stored declarative knowledge about person, task, and strategy factors affecting cognition) and (b) metacognitive experiences (real-time monitoring signals arising during cognitive engagement).
> **Source:** Section 2; Flavell (1979)
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** definition, metacognition, Flavell

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-skills-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 2 — Framework: Nelson-Narens
> **A:** **Q:** Describe the two levels and two information flows in the Nelson-Narens metacognitive monitoring-control framework.
> **A:** Object level: first-order cognitive processes (studying, reasoning, retrieving). Meta level: metacognitive processes that observe and control the object level. Upward flow (monitoring): the meta level receives information about the object level's state (via JOLs, FOK, EOL). Downward flow (control): the meta level sends commands that modify object-level processes (study more, stop, seek help).
> **Source:** Section 3
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** framework, Nelson-Narens, monitoring, control

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-skills-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 3 — Distinction: Monitoring vs. Regulation
> **A:** **Q:** How do metacognitive monitoring and regulation differ, and why does the monitoring-control gap matter?
> **A:** Monitoring is the metacognitive assessment of one's current cognitive state (JOL, FOK, comprehension check — generating information). Regulation is the behavioral response to that information (study more, switch strategies, seek help — executing change). The monitoring-control gap describes findings that accurate monitoring does not automatically translate into appropriate regulation — learners may detect a comprehension gap without changing their study approach, because regulation is a separate capacity that can fail independently of monitoring.
> **Source:** Sections 3, 5
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** monitoring, regulation, distinction, monitoring-control gap

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-skills-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 4 — Mechanism: Fluency Illusion
> **A:** **Q:** Why does rereading feel more productive than retrieval practice, and why is this feeling misleading?
> **A:** Rereading familiar material is processed fluently (quickly, easily, without retrieval failure), and the monitoring system interprets this processing fluency as evidence of deep comprehension — the fluency illusion. Retrieval practice involves frequent retrieval failure, which the monitoring system correctly interprets as difficulty but incorrectly interprets as ineffective learning. In reality, the storage-retrieval paradox means the effortful encoding of retrieval practice produces more durable memory traces, while the fluent rereading of familiar material produces superficial reactivation without strengthened storage.
> **Source:** Sections 4, 7, 8
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** fluency illusion, desirable difficulties, retrieval practice, mechanism

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-skills-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 5 — Process: Zimmerman's SRL Cycle
> **A:** **Q:** Name the three phases of Zimmerman's cyclical SRL model and identify one metacognitive process in each.
> **A:** (1) Forethought: task analysis and strategic planning before task engagement — selecting an evidence-based study strategy based on task demands and goals. (2) Performance: self-monitoring and self-instruction during task engagement — detecting comprehension failures and adjusting reading strategy in response. (3) Self-reflection: self-evaluation and causal attribution after task completion — assessing whether performance met goals and attributing gaps to modifiable (strategy, preparation) rather than fixed (ability) causes.
> **Source:** Section 5
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** Zimmerman, SRL, three-phase cycle, regulation

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-skills-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 6 — Concept: Desirable Difficulties
> **A:** **Q:** Name three desirable difficulties and explain one structural reason they improve long-term retention despite feeling less effective.
> **A:** Three desirable difficulties: (1) retrieval practice (testing rather than restudying), (2) interleaving (mixing problem types during practice), (3) spaced practice (distributing study across time). Structural reason: the storage-retrieval paradox — conditions that require effortful encoding (which creates the sensation of difficulty) produce more elaborated and robustly linked memory traces that are accessible under a wider range of retrieval cues. The difficulty signals effort during encoding, not poor learning.
> **Source:** Section 7; Bjork & Bjork (2011)
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** desirable difficulties, Bjork, retrieval practice, spacing

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-skills-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 7 — Developmental: Metacognitive Inertia
> **A:** **Q:** What is metacognitive inertia, and what is its educational implication?
> **A:** Metacognitive inertia is the developmental state in which strategies and metacognitive knowledge exist in the learner's repertoire but are not spontaneously deployed without explicit prompting (Ann Brown). Young children are paradigmatically inert: they follow a strategy when prompted but do not initiate it. Educational implication: strategy instruction must include not only the what and how but the conditional knowledge of when and why — to build the spontaneous triggering that converts inert strategy knowledge into deployed strategy use.
> **Source:** Section 6
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** Ann Brown, metacognitive inertia, development, strategy instruction

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-skills-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 8 — Failure Mode: Dunning-Kruger Mechanism
> **A:** **Q:** What metacognitive mechanism underlies the Dunning-Kruger effect, and why does it affect beginners in any domain?
> **A:** The mechanism: the metacognitive skills required to recognize a correct answer and to accurately assess one's own performance quality are the same domain-specific knowledge and skills. Low performers lack not only the domain knowledge to perform accurately but the domain knowledge to monitor their performance accurately — they cannot see what they cannot see. This is not a personality trait of incompetent people but a structural feature of early-stage learning: everyone shows the Dunning-Kruger pattern in domains where they lack expertise, because expert monitoring requires expert knowledge.
> **Source:** Section 8; Kruger & Dunning (1999)
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** Dunning-Kruger, failure modes, mechanism, monitoring

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-skills-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 9 — Distinction: Conditional vs. Declarative Metacognitive Knowledge
> **A:** **Q:** Distinguish declarative from conditional metacognitive knowledge and explain which is more predictive of strategy transfer.
> **A:** Declarative metacognitive knowledge is knowing that a strategy exists ("retrieval practice is a learning strategy"). Conditional metacognitive knowledge is knowing when and why to apply it ("retrieval practice is most beneficial when the goal is durable long-term retrieval rather than immediate recognition, and when material has been study once to initial encoding threshold"). Research (Paris, Lipson, and Wixson) shows conditional knowledge is more predictive of spontaneous strategy transfer than declarative knowledge alone — because conditional knowledge provides the context triggers and rationale that prompt spontaneous deployment in novel situations.
> **Source:** Section 4
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** conditional knowledge, declarative knowledge, transfer, strategy

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-skills-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 10 — Application: Growth Mindset as Metacognitive Intervention
> **A:** **Q:** How can growth mindset interventions be understood as metacognitive knowledge interventions?
> **A:** Growth mindset (incremental intelligence theory) interventions shift the person knowledge layer of metacognitive knowledge — specifically, the learner's model of the nature of intelligence and the modifiability of their own cognitive capacities. Entity theory (fixed intelligence) makes difficulty signals trigger threat responses (monitoring "I'm confused" → interpretation "I'm not smart enough"); incremental theory makes the same difficulty signals trigger mastery responses ("I'm confused → this is an opportunity to learn"). The metacognitive mechanism is reinterpretation of monitoring signals through a different knowledge framework, not a change in monitoring capacity itself.
> **Source:** Section 7; Blackwell et al. (2007)
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** growth mindset, Dweck, metacognitive knowledge, person knowledge

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the distinction between textbase and situation model in Kintsch's reading comprehension model?
> **A:** The textbase is a coherent propositional representation of *what the text says* — the literal semantic content of the text's sentences. The situation model is a mental representation of *what the text describes* — the state of affairs in the world or domain the text refers to. Genuine comprehension requires situation model construction; textbase representation alone supports recognition but not transfer or integration.
> **Source:** Section 2
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #comprehension #reading-theory #situation-model

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the fluency illusion and why is it motivationally significant?
> **A:** The fluency illusion is the metacognitive error of mistaking the ease of text processing (reading fluency) for depth of comprehension (situation model construction). It is motivationally significant because it prevents deployment of deeper processing strategies (if the reader believes they understand, they stop working to understand), produces false assessment confidence, and when assessment performance fails, this failure is misattributed to ability rather than monitoring — reinforcing entity theories and performance-avoidance goals.
> **Source:** Sections 2 & 7
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #metacognition #fluency-illusion #comprehension-monitoring

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What are the three basic psychological needs in SDT, and how does each apply specifically to academic reading?
> **A:** (1) **Autonomy** — the need to experience reading as self-initiated and volitionally chosen. In reading: supporting autonomy means providing genuine rationales, meaningful choice within required content, and framing reading for personal understanding rather than external compliance. (2) **Competence** — the need to experience efficacy and mastery. In reading: satisfied by successful comprehension experiences, progressive difficulty calibration, and strategy training that produces genuine understanding. (3) **Relatedness** — the need for connection with others. In reading: satisfied by shared reading practices, discussion communities, and meaningful connection of reading content to valued relationships and communities.
> **Source:** Section 4
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #SDT #basic-psychological-needs #reading-motivation

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** Distinguish mastery-approach, mastery-avoidance, performance-approach, and performance-avoidance goals in the context of academic reading.
> **A:** **Mastery-approach:** Reading to develop genuine understanding; predicts deep processing, persistence, positive affect. **Mastery-avoidance:** Reading to avoid failure-to-understand, driven by comprehension anxiety; predicts self-monitoring and persistence from fear. **Performance-approach:** Reading to demonstrate superior comprehension relative to others; predicts adequate assessed performance but surface strategic reading. **Performance-avoidance:** Reading to avoid appearing incompetent; predicts avoidance, surface processing, learned helplessness — most consistently negative outcome profile.
> **Source:** Section 5
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #achievement-goal-theory #goal-orientation #reading-motivation

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the logic of the three-tier strategy hierarchy and what does it mean to "start at the wrong tier"?
> **A:** The hierarchy sequences strategies according to prerequisite logic: Tier 1 (cognitive foundation) creates conditions Tier 2 presupposes; Tier 2 (comprehension strategies) creates conditions Tier 3 presupposes. Starting at the wrong tier means applying strategies that require conditions not yet in place. Example: teaching elaborative interrogation (Tier 2) to a reader with profound vocabulary gaps (Tier 1 deficit) will fail because the reader cannot generate meaningful elaborations when basic comprehension is blocked by unknown vocabulary. The diagnostic must precede the prescription.
> **Source:** Section 6
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #strategy-hierarchy #reading-motivation #intervention-design

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is self-explanation as a reading strategy and why is it more effective than summarization?
> **A:** Self-explanation involves generating explanatory verbalizations or written articulations of what a passage means in the reader's own words, how it relates to prior content and knowledge, and what gaps remain. It is more effective than summarization because it requires construction of causal relationships and inferential connections that the text may not state explicitly — forcing situation model construction rather than accurate textbase restatement. Summarization can be achieved at the textbase level; genuine self-explanation requires situation model access.
> **Source:** Section 6.3
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #self-explanation #comprehension-strategy #situation-model

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is Zimmerman's cyclical SRL model and what are the three phases applied to academic reading?
> **A:** **Forethought phase:** Before reading — goal setting (what do I want to genuinely understand?), strategic planning (which strategies are appropriate for this text?), motivational self-management (intrinsic goal activation, prior knowledge preparation). **Performance phase:** During reading — strategy deployment, comprehension monitoring, fix-up strategy selection and execution when failures are detected. **Self-reflection phase:** After reading — self-evaluation of comprehension quality, causal attribution of successes and failures, adaptive planning for the next reading session. The cycle makes self-regulated reading self-improving rather than repetitively consistent.
> **Source:** Section 7.2
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #self-regulated-learning #metacognition #reading-self-regulation

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is interest theory's four-phase model and what distinguishes situational from individual interest?
> **A:** Four phases: (1) Triggered situational interest — externally triggered by environmental features; (2) Maintained situational interest — sustained by engaging content value; (3) Emerging individual interest — beginning of stored value and re-engagement tendency; (4) Well-developed individual interest — stable, self-sustaining predisposition to engage. **Situational interest** is triggered by specific contextual features (an engaging text, a surprising demonstration) and dissipates when those features are absent. **Individual interest** is a relatively stable personal predisposition that motivates seeking out domain-relevant content across contexts, independent of specific environmental triggers.
> **Source:** Section 3.3
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #interest-theory #situational-interest #individual-interest #reading-motivation

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** Why is retrieval practice more effective than re-reading for long-term retention, and how should it be applied during academic reading?
> **A:** Retrieval practice is more effective because the act of retrieving strengthens the memory trace through effortful reconstruction (a desirable difficulty that enhances encoding), whereas re-reading produces recognition familiarity (high processing fluency) without effortful retrieval — it improves performance on immediate tests but not long-term retention. **Application during reading:** After completing a section, close or cover the text and write from memory the main points and key arguments. Check accuracy against the text. The gaps identified during retrieval attempts are precisely the content requiring focused re-reading or further elaboration.
> **Source:** Sections 6.3 and 7.3
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #retrieval-practice #spaced-repetition #desirable-difficulties #reading-strategy

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the Motivational-Cognitive Reading Stack and what diagnostic implication does it carry?
> **A:** The Stack integrates four systems that must each meet threshold development for motivated academic reading to be self-sustaining: **Layer 1** (Cognitive Pre-Conditions: fluency, vocabulary, working memory management, prior knowledge), **Layer 2** (Comprehension-Generating Strategies: self-explanation, elaborative interrogation, retrieval practice), **Layer 3** (Motivation Architecture: autonomous regulation, mastery goals, intrinsic goal framing, individual interest). **Diagnostic implication:** Identify the lowest layer where the system is failing. A reader who cannot sustain motivation despite good comprehension skills needs Layer 3 work. A reader who is motivated but comprehends shallowly needs Layer 2 strategy training. A reader whose strategies fail needs Layer 1 development. Starting intervention at the correct layer is essential.
> **Source:** Section 6.4 original synthesis
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #motivational-cognitive-stack #original-synthesis #reading-motivation #diagnostic

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivation-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question (Definition):** What is the Motivational Deployment Problem, and how does it differ from strategy ignorance or strategic incapacity?
> 
> **Answer:** The Motivational Deployment Problem is the structural gap between knowing effective learning strategies work and consistently deploying them. It differs from strategy ignorance (not knowing what works) and strategic incapacity (lacking the skill) in that the learner has both knowledge and capacity but fails to deploy due to motivational barriers — primarily the aversive phenomenological experience of the strategies that work best.
> 
> **Source:** Section 1: The Motivation-Strategy Nexus
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #motivation #learning-science #strategy-deployment

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivation-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question (Distinction):** How does autonomous motivation differ from controlled motivation in its effect on the SRL monitoring phase?
> 
> **Answer:** Autonomous motivation supports honest monitoring: comprehension failure is experienced as information, not threat, enabling accurate self-assessment. Controlled motivation produces ego-defensive monitoring: learners subjectively upgrade comprehension estimates under introjected regulation (where self-esteem depends on performance), filtering out monitoring signals that would damage self-concept. Same monitoring capacity; opposite willingness to attend to what it reveals.
> 
> **Source:** Section 5.2: Where SDT Intersects the SRL Cycle
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #self-determination-theory #self-regulated-learning #metacognition

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivation-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question (Process):** Walk through the hierarchical order of the seven learning effects, explaining why the ordering reflects both cognitive depth and motivational cost.
> 
> **Answer:** Generation (pause, produce) → Testing Effect (retrieve, tolerate failure) → Spacing (tolerate forgetting intervals) → Interleaving (tolerate productive confusion) → Elaboration (sustain effortful connection-making) → Self-Explanation (confront comprehension gaps) → Metacognitive Strategy (regulate all others honestly). Each requires progressively more: more working memory demand, more tolerance of aversive experience, more SRL phase engagement, and more motivational infrastructure. The hierarchical ordering means lower strategies are prerequisite motivational entry points for higher strategies.
> 
> **Source:** Section 3: The Seven Learning Strategies
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #learning-effects #hierarchy #desirable-difficulties

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivation-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question (Definition):** What is the desirable difficulties paradox, and why does it create a persistent motivational problem?
> 
> **Answer:** The desirable difficulties paradox is the finding that the conditions which feel most like productive learning (massed practice, re-reading, blocked practice) are among the least effective for long-term retention, while conditions that feel like ineffective or failed learning (spaced practice, retrieval failure, interleaving confusion) are the most effective. The persistent motivational problem is this: learners use subjective sense of learning as their primary feedback signal for strategy effectiveness, but this signal is systematically inverted for the strategies that matter most.
> 
> **Source:** Section 2.4: Cognitive Load and the Learning Process
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #desirable-difficulties #metacognitive-distortion #strategy-selection

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivation-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question (Application):** You have just completed reading a complex 5000-word report on cognitive science. Design the optimal post-reading protocol using specific learning effects to maximize long-term retention.
> 
> **Answer:** (1) **Free recall test** (testing effect at maximum): Close the report and write everything you can recall, without aids, for 15 minutes. (2) **Gap review** (generation + self-explanation): Open the report only to check against your recall; for each gap, generate a self-explanation of why you missed it and create a permanent note. (3) **Connection mapping** (elaboration): Open your vault and create bidirectional links from the new material to at least 3-5 existing permanent notes, writing 1-2 sentences explaining each connection. (4) **SRS card creation** (spacing + retrieval practice for future): Create Anki cards from the key concepts, ensuring question forms require generation rather than recognition. (5) **Set spaced review intervals**: Schedule a retrieval test at 1 day, 3 days, 1 week, 2 weeks, 1 month from reading.
> 
> **Source:** Section 7.2: Strategy Deployment in the Reading-to-PKB Workflow
> **Difficulty:** Application
> **Tags:** #reading-practice #pkb-workflow #seven-strategies

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivation-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question (Connection):** What is the relationship between the basic psychological needs (SDT) and mastery goal adoption (Achievement Goal Theory)?
> 
> **Answer:** Need satisfaction and mastery goals are bidirectionally reinforcing. Autonomy need satisfaction predicts mastery goal adoption: learners who experience their study as self-chosen and personally meaningful define success in terms of developing genuine understanding rather than social performance. Competence need satisfaction supports mastery goals by calibrating challenge to the growing edge, keeping failure informative rather than devastating. Conversely, mastery goals support need satisfaction: orienting toward competence development creates the contexts in which genuine improvement (competence need) and self-chosen challenge (autonomy need) are most likely to be experienced. Performance goals tend to frustrate both needs by making success contingent on social comparison rather than genuine development.
> 
> **Source:** Section 4.2: Achievement Goal Theory
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #self-determination-theory #achievement-goal-theory #motivation

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivation-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question (Definition):** What is the Fluency Illusion, and what is its primary consequence for self-regulated reading?
> 
> **Answer:** The fluency illusion is the systematic tendency to mistake the cognitive ease of re-reading (increased familiarity = easier processing = faster reading) for a reliable signal of learning depth. The primary consequence for self-regulated reading is monitoring corruption: the monitoring phase of the SRL cycle receives false positive signals (high comprehension estimates) from re-reading, preventing the control phase from triggering the strategy adjustments (retrieval practice, self-explanation) that would actually produce durable encoding. Learners who rely heavily on re-reading are operating with calibrated monitoring for the wrong signal.
> 
> **Source:** Section 2.5: Prior Knowledge and the Fluency Illusion
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #fluency-illusion #metacognitive-monitoring #self-regulated-learning

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivation-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question (Distinction):** Distinguish between the motivational costs of the testing effect, the spacing effect, and the interleaving effect. Why is each difficult for a different reason?
> 
> **Answer:** **Testing effect**: Motivationally costly because it provides immediate, undeniable evidence of forgetting and incompetence — it directly confronts the self-efficacy of learners with performance goals. **Spacing effect**: Motivationally costly because it requires deliberately *tolerating* forgetting between sessions — the subjective experience of apparently losing progress between reviews feels like evidence the strategy is failing, when it is actually the mechanism of its success. **Interleaving effect**: Motivationally costly because interleaved practice consistently produces *lower* subjective confidence and *lower* apparent performance than blocked practice during the session itself, even though it produces better long-term transfer. The interleaving problem is the starkest inversion of the performance-mastery feel: you feel less competent during the strategy that actually makes you most competent.
> 
> **Source:** Sections 3.2, 3.3, 3.4
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #desirable-difficulties #testing-effect #spacing-effect #interleaving

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the testing effect, and why does it produce better retention than re-studying?
> **A:** The testing effect is the finding that retrieving information from long-term memory produces stronger, more durable traces than equivalent re-studying. It works because: (1) retrieval builds the retrieval pathway specifically; (2) elaborative retrieval enriches encoding; (3) failed retrieval creates error-correction opportunities; (4) it is a desirable difficulty that engages consolidation processes.
> **Source:** Section 4, [[Testing-Effect]]
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the fluency illusion, and why is it a metacognitive problem?
> **A:** The fluency illusion is using processing ease (how smoothly text is read) as a cue for comprehension depth (how deeply it has been understood). It is a metacognitive monitoring error: the wrong signal (fluency) is feeding the judgment-of-learning system, producing systematic overconfidence in understanding and premature termination of study.
> **Source:** Sections 1 and 7, [[fluency-illusion]]
> **Difficulty:** Basic

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is a desirable difficulty, and what distinguishes it from undesirable difficulty?
> **A:** A desirable difficulty impedes performance during acquisition but produces better long-term retention and transfer by engaging constructive and reconstructive memory operations (retrieval, elaboration, discrimination). Undesirable difficulty impedes performance *without* engaging these operations — e.g., cramped fonts, poor organization, cognitive overload beyond competence zone.
> **Source:** Section 2, [[desirable-difficulties]]
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** How do spacing and retrieval practice interact, and why is the interaction important?
> **A:** Retrieval practice is substantially more powerful when combined with spacing: testing at a delay requires actual retrieval from long-term memory (implicit retrieval practice embedded in spaced study), while testing immediately draws on working memory. The combination — retrieval at optimally spaced intervals — is the theoretical foundation of spaced repetition systems.
> **Source:** Sections 4 and 5, [[spaced-repetition]], [[retrieval-practice]]
> **Difficulty:** Advanced

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the interleaving effect, and why does it feel less productive than blocked study?
> **A:** The interleaving effect is that mixing problem types or topics within a study session produces better long-term retention and transfer than studying each type exhaustively before moving to the next. It feels less productive because it prevents the shallow repetition fluency that blocked study generates — requiring re-encoding from scratch on each encounter, which is the source of both the felt difficulty and the learning benefit.
> **Source:** Section 5, [[interleaving-effect]]
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** According to Self-Determination Theory, what are the three basic psychological needs and their relevance to academic reading engagement?
> **A:** Autonomy (experiencing reading as self-chosen, identity-aligned), Competence (experiencing genuine mastery growth through challenge calibrated to current ability), and Relatedness (experiencing connection to intellectual community and purpose). All three must be satisfied for reading engagement to be sustainable and autonomously motivated rather than compliance-driven and fragile.
> **Source:** Section 8, [[basic-psychological-needs]], [[self-determination-theory]]
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the generation effect, and how does it differ from retrieval practice?
> **A:** The generation effect is the finding that self-generated information is encoded more durably than received information. Unlike retrieval practice (which retrieves already-encoded information), generation occurs *during initial encoding* — before durable storage. Both engage active construction, but at different points in the learning cycle: generation at encoding, retrieval practice during maintenance.
> **Source:** Section 3, [[generation-effect]]
> **Difficulty:** Advanced

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the self-explanation effect, and what is its primary mechanism?
> **A:** Students who generate more self-explanations during problem-solving and text study (articulating why claims are true, how processes work) show substantially greater learning gains than those who explain less. The primary mechanism is gap-detection: self-explanation forces the learner to confront logical gaps in their understanding that fluent reading conceals, triggering targeted repair of exactly the incomplete aspects of their mental model.
> **Source:** Section 6, [[self-explanation-effect]]
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What are the three phases of Zimmerman's cyclical self-regulated learning model, and what is their relevance to strategic reading?
> **A:** Forethought (goal-setting, prior knowledge activation, strategy planning before reading), Performance (active strategy deployment with comprehension monitoring during reading), and Self-Reflection (honest post-reading evaluation of what was learned and strategy effectiveness). Most natural readers skip forethought and self-reflection entirely, which eliminates the cyclical improvement mechanism that makes SRL more effective over time.
> **Source:** Section 10, [[cyclical-model-of-self-regulated-learning]]
> **Difficulty:** Advanced

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** In the Nelson-Narens model of metacognition, what are monitoring and control, and how do they interact?
> **A:** Monitoring: information flowing from the object (cognitive) level to the meta level, including judgments of learning, feelings of knowing, and comprehension signals. Control: commands flowing from the meta level to the object level, adjusting strategies, allocating study time, deciding to review. Effective self-regulation requires both: monitoring that generates accurate signals, and control operations that respond to those signals adaptively.
> **Source:** Section 7, [[nelson-narens-model]]
> **Difficulty:** Advanced










































































## Protocols & Methods

> [!protocol] **The Full Pre-Reading Frame Protocol** *(from [[situation-model-construction-focused-analysis-2026-03-29]])*
> **Context:** Use before reading any complex expository text (academic articles, technical chapters, analytical essays). Takes 3–7 minutes.
> 
> **Derived From:** Phase II (Advance Organizer function), Phase V (Implication 1), Frame-Before-Fill Asymmetry (Phase VI).
> 
> **Steps:**
> 1. **Survey Structure (2 min):** Read only headings, subheadings, the opening paragraph, and the closing paragraph of each major section. You are reading for *architecture*, not content.
> 2. **Write a Prediction Sentence (1 min):** Complete this sentence without looking at the text: "This text is going to argue/explain/demonstrate \_\_\_\_." This activates prior knowledge and initiates the structural frame.
> 3. **Set a Purpose Question (1 min):** Write: "I am reading this to understand \_\_\_\_." The more specific, the…

> [!protocol] **The Causal Spine Tracking Protocol** *(from [[situation-model-construction-focused-analysis-2026-03-29]])*
> **Context:** Use during reading of any text with an argument or mechanistic explanation. Requires a notebook or annotation margin.
> 
> **Derived From:** Phase IV (Mechanism 3: Causal Spine Bottleneck), Phase V (Implication 4).
> 
> **Steps:**
> 1. **Maintain a running causal chain** in your notes: "X → Y → Z." Each time you encounter an explicit or implicit causal claim, add it to the chain.
> 2. **At every paragraph break**, ask: "What is the causal relationship between this paragraph and the previous one?" If you cannot answer, this is a gap requiring active inference generation.
> 3. **Mark causal gap points** in the margin with a "?" — these are locations where the text does not explicitly connect, and you must bridge them yourself.
> 4. **Generate bridging inferences** at gap points: "Given what I…

> [!protocol] **Calibrate-First Formative Assessment Design Protocol** *(from [[formative-assessment-focused-analysis-2026-03-24]])*
> **Context:** Use when designing any formative assessment event — quiz, questioning sequence, self-assessment activity, peer review — where the goal is to produce durable learning gains, not merely immediate correction.
> 
> **Steps:**
> 1. **Generate the Calibration Event:** Before any feedback or information is provided, require the learner to produce an active response (retrieve, predict, explain) that will generate a monitoring signal. This could be: attempting a recall question without reference material, predicting the outcome of a worked example before seeing the solution, or writing a brief explanation of their current understanding before seeing peer or teacher feedback.
> 2. **Allow Monitoring Exposure:** Provide a brief pause (the "desirable difficulty pause") after the active response but before the correct answer or feedback is delivered. This allows the learner's monitoring system to generate a felt-sense of certainty or uncertainty about their response.
> 3. **Deliver Calibration Feedback (Not Informational Feedback):** The first piece of feedback should be simple and binary — correct/incorrect, or a comparison of the learner's prediction to the actual outcome. Do not yet provide elaborated informational feedback. The purpose is to confirm or disconfirm the monitoring system's prediction.
> 4. **Allow Integration Time:** Give the learner time to register the calibration signal before proceeding. For learners who were incorrect (the primary calibration event), this is when the monitoring system updates.
> 5. **Deliver Informational Feedback:** Now provide the full informational feedback — explanation, correct answer, elaboration of the process. At this point, the learner's monitoring system has been recalibrated by the calibration event and is in a more receptive state for information.
> 6. **Fade Progressively:** As monitoring accuracy improves (tracked via learner self-prediction accuracy over sessions), reduce the scaffolding of steps 1-4, shifting responsibility for generating calibration events to the learner through self-testing and self-assessment.
> 
> **Success Criteria:** Over multiple sessions, learner self-prediction accuracy improves (monitoring accuracy is increasing). Learners request recalibration opportunities rather than waiting for external feedback.
> 
> **Common Pitfalls:** Skipping the calibration event and providing information directly (remains in ITM mode). Providing elaborated feedback *before* the calibration signal (overwhelms the calibration event with information). Maintaining scaffolding indefinitely without fading (risks scaffold-dependence).

> [!protocol] **The Calibration Review Protocol** *(from [[metacognition-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Purpose:** Build metacognitive calibration by comparing predicted knowledge with actual knowledge during PKM review.
> 
> **Steps:**
> 1. **Select** a note or topic for review.
> 2. **Predict** (before opening the note): Write down 3-5 key points you believe the note contains. Rate your confidence (1-10) that your recall is accurate.
> 3. **Retrieve** (attempt from memory): Without looking at any notes, explain the concept in your own words. Note where your explanation feels uncertain or incomplete.
> 4. **Compare**: Open the note. Compare your prediction and retrieval with the actual content. Mark: (a) things you remembered accurately, (b) things you forgot, (c) things you remembered incorrectly, (d) connections you missed.
> 5. **Calibrate**: Compare your confidence rating with your actual accuracy. Were you overconfident? Underconfident? Identify patterns in your calibration errors.
> 6. **Log**: Record the calibration result (predicted accuracy, actual accuracy, discrepancy) in a simple tracking system.
> 
> **Frequency:** Apply to 3-5 notes per review session. Track calibration trends weekly.
> 
> **Expected outcome:** Monitoring accuracy improves over 4-8 weeks of consistent practice.

> [!protocol] **The Metacognitive Processing Template** *(from [[metacognition-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Purpose:** Scaffold deep processing of source material through structured metacognitive prompts.
> 
> **Use when:** Processing a new article, chapter, or concept that requires integration with existing knowledge.
> 
> **Template structure:**
> 1. **Pre-processing assessment** (planning regulation):
>    - What do I already know about this topic? (≥3 existing connections)
>    - What am I trying to learn from this? (specific question)
>    - What processing depth does this warrant? (skim / standard / deep)
> 2. **During processing** (monitoring regulation):
>    - Central claim or thesis: [expressed in own words]
>    - Supporting evidence: [key evidence, with quality assessment]
>    - Points of confusion: [explicit confusion markers]
>    - Connections to existing knowledge: [≥2 wiki-links with explanation]
> 3. **Post-processing evaluation** (evaluative regulation):
>    - Can I explain this to someone else without notes? [attempt]
>    - What changed in my understanding? [before/after delta]
>    - What questions remain? [specific, actionable questions]
>    - How confident am I in my understanding? [1-10, with justification]
> 
> **Design principle:** Each prompt is a regulatory instruction that activates monitoring processes the practitioner might otherwise skip.

> [!protocol] **Weekly Metacognitive Review Protocol (30 minutes)** *(from [[metacognitive-skills-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> A structured end-of-week review protocol implementing Zimmerman's self-reflection phase as a regular practice:
> 
> **Step 1 — Performance Inventory (10 minutes):**
> List 3-5 learning activities or challenges from the week. For each: What did I understand well? Where did I struggle or get confused? What monitoring signals (fluency vs. difficulty) did I have? Were those signals accurate?
> 
> **Step 2 — Strategy Audit (10 minutes):**
> For each area of struggle: What strategy was I using? Was it evidence-based? Did I apply retrieval practice, spacing, or interleaving — or did I default to re-reading/highlighting? What would I change if I could restart this week's study?
> 
> **Step 3 — Calibration Check (5 minutes):**
> If you have test results or feedback from the week: How accurate was your pre-test confidence? Did you over- or under-estimate? On which types of material does your calibration tend to be least accurate?
> 
> **Step 4 — Next Week Planning (5 minutes):**
> Set 2-3 specific metacognitive goals for next week (not content goals but monitoring and regulation goals): e.g., "Retrieve before re-reading," "Stop after each section and test understanding with a question," "Track confidence before and after review."

> [!protocol] **Metacognitive Error Log** *(from [[metacognitive-skills-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> Maintain a running log of metacognitive errors — instances where your monitoring signal was incorrect. For each entry:
> - **Date and context:** What were you learning or doing?
> - **The false signal:** What did your monitoring system tell you? (confident, confused, understanding, not understanding)
> - **The actual state:** What did subsequent testing, feedback, or application reveal?
> - **Pattern:** Is this error consistent with a known failure mode? (fluency illusion, Dunning-Kruger, overconfidence in familiar-feeling material)
> - **Adjustment:** What monitoring practice would have caught this earlier?
> 
> Reviewing this log monthly reveals systematic patterns in your personal metacognitive failure profile — enabling targeted calibration training.

> [!protocol] **Protocol 1: Pre-Reading Motivational Alignment Ritual (5 minutes)** *(from [[motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Purpose:** Activate intrinsic goal orientation before beginning a reading session.
> **Steps:**
> 1. Pause for 60 seconds. Close all other applications.
> 2. Write or verbalize: "What am I genuinely curious about in this domain?"
> 3. Write or verbalize: "What specific questions would a deeply satisfying reading of this text answer for me?"
> 4. Set a reading goal framed in mastery terms: "By the end of this session, I want to genuinely understand [specific concept or argument]."
> 5. Locate 2–3 prior knowledge hooks: "This connects to what I already know about [X, Y, Z]."
> **Frequency:** Before every academic reading session.

> [!protocol] **Protocol 2: Tier 2 Active Reading Cycle (Per-Section Loop)** *(from [[motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Purpose:** Ensure situation model construction rather than textbase-level reading.
> **Steps (per section or major subsection):**
> 1. **Pre-read:** Scan headings and first sentences. Generate one genuine question.
> 2. **Read:** Text segment at natural reading pace. Annotate: ? for confusions, ! for surprises, * for key claims.
> 3. **Stop:** Close or cover the text.
> 4. **Retrieve:** Write from memory: What were the 2–3 main points? What was the key argument?
> 5. **Self-explain:** Explain in your own words *why* the argument is structured as it is and how it connects to what came before.
> 6. **Check:** Re-read to verify retrieval accuracy. Note gaps.
> 7. **Forward-connect:** One sentence connecting this section to upcoming or broader material.
> **Frequency:** Every section/subsection during academic reading.

> [!protocol] **The Complete Strategic Reading Session Protocol** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Before Reading (10–15 minutes):**
> - [ ] Identify learning goal: What should you understand/be able to do after reading?
> - [ ] Activate prior knowledge: 5-minute free recall of what you already know about the topic
> - [ ] Review 2–3 related PKB notes for schema warm-up
> - [ ] Generate 3–5 advance questions from headings and abstract
> 
> **During Reading (adapted to text length and density):**
> - [ ] Look-away note-making after each major subsection
> - [ ] Online comprehension monitoring (flag unclear passages with "?")
> - [ ] Self-explanation annotations for key claims ("this is true because...")
> - [ ] Generative connection notes ("this connects to [[Related-Concept]] because...")
> 
> **After Reading (15–20 minutes):**
> - [ ] Immediate free recall: write everything remembered without looking at notes
> - [ ] Compare recall to source text; identify gaps
> - [ ] Create/update atomic concept notes in PKB from recall + gap-filled source review
> - [ ] Build wiki-link connections for each new note (minimum 2 per note)
> - [ ] Mark flashcard seeds in notes for SRS queue
> - [ ] Metacognitive self-rating: comprehension depth (1–5), strategy effectiveness (1–5)









## Visual Representations

> [!diagram] **Core Argument Structure: Why Readers Lose the Big Picture** *(from [[situation-model-construction-focused-analysis-2026-03-29]])*
> ```
> PREMISE 1: Reading produces three levels of representation
>   Surface Code → Textbase → Situation Model
>                              (what the text is ABOUT)
> 
> PREMISE 2: Situation models require active construction
>   Active inference generation → Causal tracking → CATSM coherence
> 
> PREMISE 3: Passive reading does not force active construction
>   Fluency → False comprehension signal → Effort termination
> 
> CONSEQUENCE: The Failure Cascade
>   [Working Memory Load] → [Textbase built automatically]
>          ↓
>   [Fluency Illusion fires] → [Comprehension effort stops]
>          ↓
>   [Causal Spine gaps remain unbridged]
>          ↓
>   [CATSM incoherence — "lost in details"]
> 
> ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════
>   SYNTHESIS: Frame-Before-Fill Asymmetry
>   All four failure modes = absence of structural frame
>   All four interventions = frame construction or maintenance
> ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════
> 
> INTERVENTIONS (mapped to mechanisms):
>   Pre-reading scaffold → builds frame before content (Mech 1, 3)
>   Own-words pause → forces generation (Mech 4)
>   Causal tracking → maintains spine (Mech 3)
>   Post-reading recall → externalizes and tests model (Mech 2)
> ```
> **Reading Guide:** The cascade in the middle shows how the four mechanisms compound into a single failure experience. The synthesis box shows the unifying principle. The interventions at the bottom map directly onto specific mechanisms — each intervention addresses a specific mechanism rather than being a general "try harder" instruction.

> [!diagram] **Core Argument Structure: ITM vs. MCM** *(from [[formative-assessment-focused-analysis-2026-03-24]])*
> ```
> ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
> │              THE FORMATIVE ASSESSMENT PARADOX               │
> │     Strong evidence base + Persistent implementation gap    │
> └───────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────┘
>                         │
>              ┌──────────┴──────────┐
>              ▼                     ▼
> ┌────────────────────┐   ┌──────────────────────┐
> │ Information        │   │ Metacognitive         │
> │ Transfer Model     │   │ Calibration Model     │
> │                    │   │                       │
> │ Operative var:     │   │ Operative var:        │
> │ Information        │   │ Monitoring accuracy   │
> │ quality &          │   │ (calibration          │
> │ accessibility      │   │ receptivity)          │
> └────────────┬───────┘   └──────────┬────────────┘
>              │                       │
>              ▼                       ▼
> ┌────────────────────┐   ┌──────────────────────┐
> │ Design lever:      │   │ Design lever:         │
> │ Better assessment  │   │ Calibration-event     │
> │ tools & clearer    │   │ density (retrieval,   │
> │ feedback           │   │ prediction, peer      │
> │                    │   │ explanation)          │
> └────────────┬───────┘   └──────────┬────────────┘
>              │                       │
>              ▼                       ▼
> ┌────────────────────┐   ┌──────────────────────┐
> │ Predicts:          │   │ Predicts:             │
> │ More/clearer info  │   │ Implementation gap    │
> │ = more gains       │   │ explained by fluency  │
> │                    │   │ illusion +            │
> │ Cannot explain:    │   │ miscalibration        │
> │ implementation gap │   │                       │
> └────────────────────┘   └──────────────────────┘
>                                    │
>                                    ▼
>                    ┌──────────────────────────────┐
>                    │   CALIBRATION-INTEGRATED     │
>                    │   MODEL (This Report)         │
>                    │                              │
>                    │   Effectiveness = f(Info ×   │
>                    │   Calibration Receptivity)   │
>                    │                              │
>                    │   Design Sequence:            │
>                    │   Calibrate First →           │
>                    │   Inform Second              │
>                    └──────────────────────────────┘
> ```
> **Reading Guide:** The argument flows top-to-bottom. The paradox (established evidence + implementation gap) motivates comparing the two models. The ITM cannot explain the gap; the MCM predicts it. The Calibration-Integrated Model synthesizes both, specifying a multiplicative relationship and a design sequence.

> [!diagram] **The Monitoring-Regulation-Calibration Triad** *(from [[metacognition-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> ```
>                    ┌─────────────────────┐
