> 2. The confidence-competence asymmetry (Dunning-Kruger) demonstrates that metacognitive limitations are self-concealing: those who most need metacognitive correction are least capable of spontaneously performing the self-assessments that would generate it.
> 3. Hattie's synthesis and expertise research both converge on metacognitive instruction as among the highest-leverage educational interventions, with effects that transfer across domains in ways content knowledge alone does not.

> [!section-summary] **Section 2 Key Takeaways** *(from [[metacognitive-skills-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> 1. Metacognition is a two-component construct: a knowledge dimension (person, task, and strategy knowledge) and a regulatory dimension (planning, monitoring, and evaluation) united by their common object — cognition itself.
> 2. Metacognitive experiences — the felt sense of knowing, not-knowing, comprehension, and confusion — are important monitoring signals but are systematically unreliable; the fluency illusion demonstrates that ease of processing misleads self-assessment of learning.
> 3. The domain-specificity versus domain-generality debate is resolved by a nuanced position: general regulatory processes exist and transfer, but their expression requires domain-specific knowledge that gives monitoring content and strategy selection meaning.

> [!section-summary] **Section 3 Key Takeaways** *(from [[metacognitive-skills-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> 1. The Nelson-Narens framework organizes metacognition into monitoring (upward information flow from object level to meta level) and control (downward information flow implementing regulatory changes), with all metacognitive phenomena fitting within this architecture.
> 2. Specific monitoring processes — ease of learning judgments, judgments of learning, feelings of knowing, and confidence judgments — each have characteristic biases and accuracy profiles that have direct practical implications for study strategy design.
> 3. The monitoring-control gap — failing to act on correct monitoring information — may be a more common failure mode than monitoring inaccuracy, implicating volitional and motivational dimensions of regulation beyond purely cognitive skill.

> [!section-summary] **Section 4 Key Takeaways** *(from [[metacognitive-skills-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> 1. Metacognitive knowledge is organized into three subcategories: person knowledge (about self and others as cognitive beings), task knowledge (about how task features influence demands), and strategy knowledge (about when and why strategies are effective).
> 2. Paris's declarative-procedural-conditional taxonomy extends Flavell's framework: declarative knowledge (knowing what), procedural knowledge (knowing how), and conditional knowledge (knowing when and why) must all be developed for metacognitive skill to be fully enacted.
> 3. The strategy knowledge gap — students' systematic overestimation of low-efficacy strategies (rereading, highlighting) and underestimation of high-efficacy strategies (retrieval practice, distributed practice) — is one of the most consequential documented failures of metacognitive knowledge, and one of the most directly addressable through explicit instruction.

> [!section-summary] **Section 5 Key Takeaways** *(from [[metacognitive-skills-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> 1. Metacognitive regulation operates through three phases: forethought (planning before engagement), performance monitoring (ongoing during engagement), and self-reflection (evaluation and updating after engagement), forming the cyclical structure of Zimmerman's SRL model.
> 2. Comprehension monitoring — detecting and correcting reading comprehension failures — is the most extensively studied instance of metacognitive regulation, and reciprocal teaching is its most empirically validated training approach.
> 3. The prefrontal cortex implements metacognitive regulation neurologically; external metacognitive scaffolds can substitute for underdeveloped internal regulation, functioning as developmental bridges to internalized metacognitive capacity.

> [!section-summary] **Section 6 Key Takeaways** *(from [[metacognitive-skills-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> 1. Metacognitive development begins with theory of mind in early childhood and progresses through the differentiation of metacognitive knowledge types, the development of spontaneous strategy deployment (overcoming metacognitive inertia), and, in mature form, epistemological reflection on the frameworks by which knowledge is evaluated.
> 2. Expertise development produces qualitatively different metacognitive monitoring content: novices monitor surface features while experts monitor deep structural features, a difference that both reflects and creates the performance gap between levels.
> 3. Metacognitive development is both maturational and educational — biological maturation provides the substrate, but explicit instruction, feedback, and metacognitive scaffolding are required to actualize developmental potential, and their absence can maintain metacognitive naivety into adulthood.

> [!section-summary] **Section 7 Key Takeaways** *(from [[metacognitive-skills-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> 1. Metacognitive skills are trainable, but training must include rationale (conditional knowledge) to produce durable strategy adoption and transfer; strategy instruction without the "why" reliably fails to generalize.
> 2. Desirable difficulties — retrieval practice, interleaving, and spaced practice — are high-leverage metacognitive training targets because they realign learners' metacognitive monitoring preferences with actual learning mechanisms, overcoming the fluency illusion.
> 3. Growth mindset interventions are metacognitive person-knowledge interventions that shift how learners interpret monitoring signals of difficulty, with documented effects on academic trajectories that can propagate from a single educational intervention.

> [!section-summary] **Section 8 Key Takeaways** *(from [[metacognitive-skills-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> 1. Metacognitive failures are systematic and predictable: the Dunning-Kruger effect, the illusion of knowing, and metacognitive blindness each arise from specific structural features of the monitoring system — not from personal failings or random errors.
> 2. The illusion of knowing is the most consequential failure for learning: it exploits the conflation of processing fluency with comprehension depth, leading to systematic preference for study strategies that feel productive (rereading) over those that actually produce durable learning (retrieval practice).
> 3. The deepest metacognitive challenge is developing meta-meta-cognitive humility — a stable, calibrated belief that one's own monitoring system is fallible and opaque in predictable ways — which enables external feedback-seeking to compensate for internal monitoring limitations.

> [!section-summary] **Section 1 Takeaways** *(from [[motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> 1. Motivated academic reading is defined by regulatory quality and processing depth, not text source or assigned status — it is malleable and subject to intervention.
> 2. Academic texts are structurally motivationally challenging because they simultaneously threaten competence, erode autonomy, and disrupt relatedness along all three basic psychological needs.
> 3. The stakes are significant: motivated academic reading is a foundational skill for lifelong learning, PKB productivity, and epistemic independence.

> [!section-summary] **Section 2 Takeaways** *(from [[motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> 1. Genuine reading comprehension requires situation model construction — a multidimensional mental simulation of the text's referential world — not merely textbase recall. Academic reading motivation that fails to produce situation model construction is cognitively shallow regardless of enrollment or time investment.
> 2. Domain-specific prior knowledge is the strongest predictor of comprehension quality because it enables the inferential connections necessary for situation model construction. This creates a bootstrapping problem that entry-level scaffolding must explicitly address.
> 3. Working memory constraints set cognitive ceilings on comprehension quality that motivation cannot override. Effective intervention combines load management with motivational scaffolding to create conditions where deep processing is both desired and cognitively feasible.

> [!section-summary] **Section 3 Takeaways** *(from [[motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> 1. Reading motivation is irreducibly multidimensional; effective intervention requires targeting specific motivational components (self-efficacy, interest, value, cost) rather than generic "motivation enhancement."
> 2. Interest in academic reading can be actively generated rather than passively awaited through deliberate text selection and epistemic stance cultivation — treating interest as a strategy, not just a personality trait.
> 3. Expectancy-value framing reveals multiple leverage points for reading motivation intervention, particularly utility value and cost reduction, which are often more tractable than intrinsic value for assigned academic content.

> [!section-summary] **Section 4 Takeaways** *(from [[motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> 1. SDT uniquely addresses the quality dimension of reading motivation, predicting that autonomous regulation produces qualitatively different reading behavior (deeper strategies, more persistence, higher transfer) compared to controlled regulation regardless of motivational intensity.
> 2. All three basic psychological needs have specific reading-environmental expressions, and the chronic failure to satisfy any of them will undermine reading motivation development — making comprehensive environmental design essential.
> 3. The internalization metabolism model identifies three conditions necessary for reading motivation to develop from external compliance toward autonomous engagement: personal relevance, competence support, and relational embedding.

> [!section-summary] **Section 5 Takeaways** *(from [[motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> 1. Goal orientation is a proximal determinant of academic reading quality: mastery goals predict deep processing and persistence; performance-avoidance goals predict surface processing and avoidance. Goal orientation diagnosis should precede strategy prescription.
> 2. The 2×2 framework reveals that performance-approach goals sometimes produce adequate achievement in assessed contexts while simultaneously undermining genuine comprehension development — a distinction critical for learners whose primary goal is understanding rather than certification.
> 3. Implicit theories of reading ability directly modulate achievement goal orientation: entity theory promotes performance-avoidance and helplessness under difficulty; incremental theory promotes mastery goals and strategic adaptation. Belief-change interventions targeting reading ability malleability are legitimate and practical.

> [!section-summary] **Section 6 Takeaways** *(from [[motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> 1. The three-tier strategy hierarchy (Cognitive Foundation → Comprehension-Oriented → Motivational Architecture) sequences interventions according to prerequisite logic: lower tiers create conditions that higher tiers presuppose. Starting at the wrong tier produces wasted effort.
> 2. Tier 2 strategies — particularly self-explanation, elaborative interrogation, and retrieval practice — are among the most robustly evidence-supported learning strategies across the empirical literature and produce direct motivational benefits through competence need satisfaction.
> 3. Tier 3 strategies address regulatory quality and goal orientation rather than cognitive technique; their effects are more diffuse but more fundamental, shaping the motivational architecture that determines whether Tier 1 and Tier 2 strategies are persistently deployed over extended reading programs.

> [!section-summary] **Section 7 Takeaways** *(from [[motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> 1. Expert reading depends on continuous metacognitive monitoring that operates at the meta-level, tracking comprehension quality and initiating adaptive responses (fix-up strategies) when failures are detected. Less-skilled readers' primary deficit is often metacognitive rather than cognitive: they do not monitor accurately enough to detect comprehension failures before false confidence sets in.
> 2. Zimmerman's cyclical SRL model provides a practical architecture for reading self-regulation: forethought phase (goal-setting, strategic planning, motivational alignment), performance phase (strategy deployment, comprehension monitoring, fix-up), and self-reflection phase (self-evaluation, attribution, adaptive planning). Each phase requires deliberate skill development.
> 3. Calibration — the alignment of subjective confidence with actual comprehension — is the metacognitive property most directly protective against fluency-illusion-driven study inefficiency. Retrieval practice is simultaneously the most effective calibration training method and the most effective comprehension-strengthening strategy.

> [!section-summary] **Section 8 Takeaways** *(from [[motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> 1. Motivated academic reading is an emergent property of four interacting systems: cognitive capacity, processing strategies, motivational orientation, and metacognitive monitoring. Each can be a limiting factor regardless of the others' development.
> 2. The virtuous cycle (comprehension → competence satisfaction → motivation → continued reading → deeper comprehension) can only operate when all four systems meet minimum threshold levels. The vicious cycle operates when the cognitive or motivational system is below threshold, requiring deliberate identification and targeted intervention at the weakest link.
> 3. The developmental trajectory has five ordered stages, each addressing prerequisite conditions for the next. Adults returning to academic reading or entering new domains begin at Stage 1 regardless of their general reading sophistication, because domain-specific cognitive prerequisites must be built before domain-specific strategies and motivational architectures can operate.

> [!section-summary] **Section 1 Summary** *(from [[motivation-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> The Motivational Deployment Problem describes the persistent gap between knowing effective learning strategies and systematically using them. This gap is produced by three intersecting forces: the phenomenological asymmetry between effective and ineffective strategies (effective ones feel worse); the incentive misalignment in formal educational environments (performance goals reward appearance of competence); and the self-efficacy trap (those most in need of desirable difficulties find them most threatening). The seven learning effects addressed in this report form a hierarchy of both cognitive demand and motivational prerequisite.

> [!section-summary] **Section 2 Summary** *(from [[motivation-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> Human memory is reconstructive and retrieval-dependent, not photographic. Encoding depth (semantic processing over surface processing) is the primary determinant of initial memory trace strength. The desirable difficulties framework explains why the seven learning effects work: they impose conditions that slow apparent performance while strengthening genuine learning through deeper encoding and retrieval-based reconsolidation. Cognitive Load Theory provides a diagnostic tool for calibrating strategy difficulty to current expertise level, with an important CLT-motivation interface showing that aversive cognitive states activate avoidance motivation under controlled regulation but function as engagement signals under autonomous motivation. Prior knowledge critically moderates strategy effectiveness, and the fluency illusion is the primary cognitive trap that passive reading enables.

> [!section-summary] **Section 3 Summary** *(from [[motivation-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> The seven learning effects form a hierachical structure based on cognitive depth and motivational cost. Generation (modest cost, meaningful benefit) provides the foundation. The testing effect requires tolerating retrieval failure. Spacing requires tolerating forgetting. Interleaving requires tolerating productive confusion. Elaboration requires sustained effortful thinking. Self-explanation requires confronting comprehension gaps. Metacognitive strategy requires maintaining honest self-monitoring across all of the above. The hierarchy is not merely descriptive — it is prescriptive for motivational system design: foundational strategies require fewer motivational prerequisites and serve as entry points that build the capacity for later strategies.

> [!section-summary] **Section 4 Summary** *(from [[motivation-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> Three motivational frameworks jointly explain strategy deployment: SDT identifies motivational quality (autonomous vs. controlled) as the primary predictor of deep strategy engagement; Achievement Goal Theory identifies mastery vs. performance goal orientation as the critical framing variable; and three additional moderators — self-efficacy, attribution style, and academic emotions — determine whether the right motivational quality and goals are sufficient to sustain strategy use under conditions of difficulty and failure. A motivational system for deep learning must attend to all three layers: cultivating autonomous motivation via need satisfaction, developing mastery goal orientation via goal-framing, and managing the moderators through specific practices and environment design.

> [!section-summary] **Section 5 Summary** *(from [[motivation-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> The SRL-SDT interface reveals that motivational quality acts as a meta-regulator of the SRL cycle, determining not just willingness to self-regulate but the honesty, adaptiveness, and effectiveness of regulation itself at each phase. Autonomous motivation supports honest monitoring, adaptive control, and growth-focused reflection; controlled motivation systematically distorts all three. Building a motivational system for deep strategy use therefore requires specifically cultivating autonomous motivational quality, not simply increasing motivational intensity. Critical transitions — failure encounters, effort-gratification gaps, and identity inflection points — are the specific moments where motivational orientation is most fragile and most transformable.

> [!section-summary] **Section 6 Summary** *(from [[motivation-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> Building a motivational system for deep reading strategy use requires three design layers: (1) environment design for basic psychological need satisfaction — deliberately structuring the social, physical, and cognitive environment to maximize autonomy, competence, and relatedness; (2) goal reframing — installing comprehension rather than coverage as the primary success criterion, and mastery rather than performance as the primary goal orientation; (3) motivational framing for each strategy — adopting accurate cognitive appraisals that preserve phenomenological honesty while transforming motivational significance. The implementation plan across daily, weekly, and long-term time scales instantiates these three layers in concrete scheduling and behavioral practice.

> [!section-summary] **Section 7 Summary** *(from [[motivation-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> The PKB architecture is a natural ally of evidence-based learning strategy deployment, with its emphasis on generation, elaboration, and connection making. The reading-to-PKB workflow can be upgraded at each phase — pre-reading, during reading, post-reading, and delayed review — to systematically implement the seven learning effects. The primary motivational challenge in PKB reading is not engagement motivation but disciplined-method motivation: sustaining the generative, self-explanatory, retrieval-based approach over the comfortable alternative of passive forward reading. The motivational system of Sections 5-6 applies directly and provides concrete tools for this challenge.

> [!section-summary] **Section 1 Takeaways** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> 1. Passive reading systematically fails to produce durable learning because it relies on re-reading and familiarity (fluency) rather than retrieval and construction — producing the [[fluency-illusion]] while accumulating minimal genuine knowledge.
> 2. The Dunlosky et al. (2013) comprehensive review empirically confirmed that the most commonly used strategies (re-reading, highlighting) have low utility, while retrieval practice and spaced practice have high utility.
> 3. The seven evidence-based strategies — generation, testing, spacing, interleaving, elaboration, self-explanation, metacognition — are not additive additions but an architecturally interdependent system that, when implemented within a motivationally sound PKB, transforms reading from passive consumption into compounding knowledge construction.

> [!section-summary] **Section 2 Takeaways** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> 1. Reading comprehension is constrained by [[working-memory]] capacity (CLT), organized through schema structures in [[long-term-memory]] (schema theory), depth-determined (Levels of Processing), and context-sensitive (encoding specificity). A strategic reading system must be designed to address all four dimensions simultaneously.
> 2. The [[Situation-Models|situation model]] is the gold standard of reading comprehension — the integrated mental representation that merges text propositions with prior knowledge. Most passive reading produces only textbase comprehension; strategic reading strategies (generation, self-explanation, elaboration) are mechanisms for ensuring situation model construction.
> 3. [[desirable-difficulties|Desirable difficulties]] — the paradoxical finding that conditions that impair current performance (spacing, retrieval, interleaving) enhance long-term retention — is the theoretical linchpin explaining why the seven evidence-based strategies outperform intuitive but ineffective passive approaches.

> [!section-summary] **Section 3 Takeaways** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> 1. The [[generation-effect]] — robust across decades of research — demonstrates that self-generated information is encoded more deeply and retained more durably than passively received information, via mechanisms of effortful processing, distinctive encoding, and prediction-error signals.
> 2. Generation strategies for academic reading include pre-reading prediction, generative titling, look-away reformulation, and blanking-and-completion techniques, all of which convert passive reception into active construction.
> 3. A PKB built around generative note-making rather than note-taking treats every note as a self-authored knowledge artifact — the primary encoding act of the reading session — and is thus a genuine implementation of the generation principle at the system level.

> [!section-summary] **Section 4 Takeaways** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> 1. The [[Testing-Effect]] — retrieval practice producing superior long-term retention compared to equivalent re-study time — is one of the most robust findings in learning science, driven by retrieval-induced strengthening, elaborative retrieval, error correction, and desirable difficulty effects.
> 2. PKB implementations of retrieval practice range from formal spaced flashcard systems ([[spaced-repetition-systems]]) to informal free recall sessions, note interrogation protocols, and retrieval-first re-reading, each with distinct advantages and appropriate use contexts.
> 3. Beyond retention improvement, retrieval practice produces a critical second-order benefit: improved [[metacognitive-calibration|metacognitive calibration]] — the accuracy of self-assessments about knowledge state — which is the foundational condition for effective self-regulated study.

> [!section-summary] **Section 5 Takeaways** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> 1. The [[spacing-effect]] — one of the most reliable findings in cognitive science — demonstrates that distributing practice across time produces dramatically better long-term retention than massing equivalent practice, via mechanisms of implicit retrieval, encoding variability, and memory consolidation windows.
> 2. [[interleaving-effect|Interleaved practice]] outperforms blocked practice on delayed tests and transfer problems despite feeling worse and performing worse in-session, because interleaving builds the discriminative recognition and retrieval skills that transfer actually demands.
> 3. Spacing and interleaving are unified expressions of **encoding diversity** — both create more robust, context-independent memories by ensuring that each encounter with material occurs under varied cognitive conditions.

> [!section-summary] **Section 6 Takeaways** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> 1. [[elaboration]] — actively connecting new information to existing knowledge through why/how/what-if questions — produces deep, transferable encodings by integrating new nodes into existing schema networks, with elaborative interrogation being one of the highest-utility study strategies.
> 2. The [[self-explanation-effect]] demonstrates that self-explanation during study produces dramatically better learning than passive reading, because it forces construction of accurate situation models and — crucially — surfaces the gaps in comprehension that fluent reading conceals.
> 3. Elaboration and self-explanation operate in opposite directions: elaboration builds the relational network (outward to existing knowledge), while self-explanation builds the conceptual model (inward to mechanism and logic), and both are necessary for deep, transferable understanding.

> [!section-summary] **Section 7 Takeaways** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> 1. The [[nelson-narens-model]] frames metacognition as a monitoring-control system, with monitoring (object → meta level) providing information about cognitive state and control (meta → object level) adjusting cognitive operations in response — both components necessary for effective self-regulation.
> 2. The key monitoring operations — [[judgment-of-learning]], [[feeling-of-knowing]], comprehension monitoring, and calibration — are systematically distorted by the fluency illusion, causing most readers to systematically overestimate their comprehension and terminate study too early.
> 3. A PKB can function as external metacognitive infrastructure, externalizing and persisting the monitoring data that fallible internal metacognition provides transiently — converting the reading system into a genuine monitoring-and-control system operating across time.

> [!section-summary] **Section 8 Takeaways** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> 1. [[self-determination-theory]] provides the motivational architecture for sustainable reading engagement: autonomous motivation (identified, integrated, intrinsic) predicts consistent strategy use and long-term engagement more reliably than effort-based or disciplinary models.
> 2. The three [[basic-psychological-needs]] — autonomy (self-chosen activity), competence (genuine mastery growth), and relatedness (connection to community and purpose) — are design constraints for any reading system intended to be used consistently over years rather than abandoned under pressure.
> 3. A PKB designed around SDT principles builds a *reading life* — a self-sustaining cycle of autonomous engagement, competence development, and growing intellectual identity — rather than merely an efficient individual study session protocol.

> [!section-summary] **Section 9 Takeaways** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> 1. A PKB for strategic reading requires a taxonomy of note types — source notes, concept notes, question notes, elaboration notes, synthesis notes — each serving distinct cognitive functions in the reading-to-knowledge pipeline.
> 2. The wiki-link knowledge graph is not mere organization — it is externalized semantic memory, and link diversity (connecting from multiple conceptual angles) implements the encoding specificity principle, building more retrievable, transferable knowledge.
> 3. SRS integration converts the PKB from an archive into a learning system; the most sustainable implementation embeds flashcard seed generation within the note-making process itself rather than requiring a separate workflow step.

> [!section-summary] **Section 10 Takeaways** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> 1. [[barry-zimmerman]]'s cyclical SRL model provides the temporal scaffold: forethought (goal-setting, prior knowledge activation, advance questions) before; generative note-making, comprehension monitoring, and self-explanation during; free recall, PKB note-making, connection-building, and SRS submission after.
> 2. The before-during-after workflow sequences the seven strategies at their points of maximum effectiveness: generation and schema activation before; elaboration and self-explanation during; retrieval practice, spacing, and metacognitive evaluation after.
> 3. Strategic reading takes more time than passive reading on a per-session basis — but produces dramatically superior outcomes on any meaningful metric: retention, transfer, metacognitive calibration, and cumulative knowledge graph richness.

## References

- **Kintsch, W. (1988). The role of knowledge in discourse comprehension: A construction-integration model. *Psychological Review*, 95(2), 163–182.**: The definitive presentation of the Construction-Integration model, providing the computational framework for understanding how three levels of text representation are built. Phases II and IV of this report draw heavily on this source.

**Recommended Sections:** The section on situation models (pp. 170–175) is the most directly relevant to this report's analytical framework.

**Historical Significance:** This paper transformed reading comprehension research from a description-level field into a mechanistically grounded computational science.
- **van Dijk, T. A., & Kintsch, W. (1983). *Strategies in Discourse Comprehension*. Academic Press.**: The foundational book-length treatment of discourse comprehension strategy, introducing the textbase/situation model distinction in its fully developed form and providing extensive evidence for the three-level architecture.

**Recommended Sections:** Chapter 2 (propositional representation), Chapter 5 (situation models), and Chapter 11 (individual differences in comprehension strategy).
- **Zwaan, R. A., & Radvansky, G. A. (1998). Situation models in language comprehension and memory. *Psychological Bulletin*, 123(2), 162–185.**: The definitive review of Event Indexing Model research, synthesizing experimental evidence for the five CATSM dimensions. Central to the Phase II framework and the Causal Spine Bottleneck analysis in Phase IV.

**Recommended Sections:** The section on causal dimension tracking (pp. 171–175) is directly relevant to the Causal Spine Bottleneck analysis.
- **Chi, M. T. H., Bassok, M., Lewis, M. W., Reimann, P., & Glaser, R. (1989). Self-explanations: How students study and use examples in learning to solve problems. *Cognitive Science*, 13(2), 145–182.**: The landmark study establishing the self-explanation effect. Provides the primary empirical basis for the Generation Constraint argument in Phases III and IV.

**Recommended Sections:** The analysis of "good" versus "poor" self-explanations (pp. 163–172) is particularly relevant.
- **Karpicke, J. D., & Roediger, H. L. (2008). The critical importance of retrieval for learning. *Science*, 319(5865), 966–968.**: The influential study documenting that retrieval practice (free recall) produces substantially better learning than re-reading, providing the evidence for the Fluency Illusion analysis and the post-reading recall intervention.
- **Graesser, A. C., Singer, M., & Trabasso, T. (1994). Constructing inferences during narrative text comprehension. *Psychological Review*, 101(3), 371–395.**: A comprehensive review and theoretical synthesis of inference generation research during reading. Central to the Generation Deficit analysis in Phase IV.
- **McNamara, D. S., & Kintsch, W. (1996). Learning from texts: Effects of prior knowledge and text coherence. *Discourse Processes*, 22(3), 247–288.**: Documents the interaction between prior knowledge and text coherence in situation model construction, providing evidence for the prior knowledge bootstrapping analysis.
- **Recht, D. R., & Leslie, L. (1988). Effect of prior knowledge on good and poor readers' memory of text. *Journal of Educational Psychology*, 80(1), 16–20.**: The baseball knowledge study demonstrating that domain knowledge overrides reading ability in predicting comprehension performance. Essential evidence for the prior knowledge dimension of situation model construction.
- **Slamecka, N. J., & Graf, P. (1978). The generation effect: Delineation of a phenomenon. *Journal of Experimental Psychology: Human Learning and Memory*, 4(6), 592–604.**: The original generation effect study. Foundational to the generation constraint analysis.
- **Ausubel, D. P. (1960). The use of advance organizers in the learning and retention of meaningful verbal material. *Journal of Educational Psychology*, 51(5), 267–272.**: The original advance organizer study. Provides the empirical basis for the pre-reading frame protocol.

*Citations sourced from [[situation-model-construction-focused-analysis-2026-03-29]]*

- **Kintsch, W. (1988). The role of knowledge in discourse comprehension: A construction-integration model. *Psychological Review*, 95(2), 163–182.**: The definitive presentation of the Construction-Integration model, providing the computational framework for understanding how three levels of text representation are built. Phases II and IV of this report draw heavily on this source.

**Recommended Sections:** The section on situation models (pp. 170–175) is the most directly relevant to this report's analytical framework.

**Historical Significance:** This paper transformed reading comprehension research from a description-level field into a mechanistically grounded computational science.

- **van Dijk, T. A., & Kintsch, W. (1983). *Strategies in Discourse Comprehension*. Academic Press.**: The foundational book-length treatment of discourse comprehension strategy, introducing the textbase/situation model distinction in its fully developed form and providing extensive evidence for the three-level architecture.

**Recommended Sections:** Chapter 2 (propositional representation), Chapter 5 (situation models), and Chapter 11 (individual differences in comprehension strategy).

- **Zwaan, R. A., & Radvansky, G. A. (1998). Situation models in language comprehension and memory. *Psychological Bulletin*, 123(2), 162–185.**: The definitive review of Event Indexing Model research, synthesizing experimental evidence for the five CATSM dimensions. Central to the Phase II framework and the Causal Spine Bottleneck analysis in Phase IV.

**Recommended Sections:** The section on causal dimension tracking (pp. 171–175) is directly relevant to the Causal Spine Bottleneck analysis.

- **Chi, M. T. H., Bassok, M., Lewis, M. W., Reimann, P., & Glaser, R. (1989). Self-explanations: How students study and use examples in learning to solve problems. *Cognitive Science*, 13(2), 145–182.**: The landmark study establishing the self-explanation effect. Provides the primary empirical basis for the Generation Constraint argument in Phases III and IV.

**Recommended Sections:** The analysis of "good" versus "poor" self-explanations (pp. 163–172) is particularly relevant.

- **Karpicke, J. D., & Roediger, H. L. (2008). The critical importance of retrieval for learning. *Science*, 319(5865), 966–968.**: The influential study documenting that retrieval practice (free recall) produces substantially better learning than re-reading, providing the evidence for the Fluency Illusion analysis and the post-reading recall intervention.

- **Graesser, A. C., Singer, M., & Trabasso, T. (1994). Constructing inferences during narrative text comprehension. *Psychological Review*, 101(3), 371–395.**: A comprehensive review and theoretical synthesis of inference generation research during reading. Central to the Generation Deficit analysis in Phase IV.

- **McNamara, D. S., & Kintsch, W. (1996). Learning from texts: Effects of prior knowledge and text coherence. *Discourse Processes*, 22(3), 247–288.**: Documents the interaction between prior knowledge and text coherence in situation model construction, providing evidence for the prior knowledge bootstrapping analysis.

- **Recht, D. R., & Leslie, L. (1988). Effect of prior knowledge on good and poor readers' memory of text. *Journal of Educational Psychology*, 80(1), 16–20.**: The baseball knowledge study demonstrating that domain knowledge overrides reading ability in predicting comprehension performance. Essential evidence for the prior knowledge dimension of situation model construction.

- **Slamecka, N. J., & Graf, P. (1978). The generation effect: Delineation of a phenomenon. *Journal of Experimental Psychology: Human Learning and Memory*, 4(6), 592–604.**: The original generation effect study. Foundational to the generation constraint analysis.

- **Ausubel, D. P. (1960). The use of advance organizers in the learning and retention of meaningful verbal material. *Journal of Educational Psychology*, 51(5), 267–272.**: The original advance organizer study. Provides the empirical basis for the pre-reading frame protocol.

- **Black, P., & Wiliam, D. (1998). Assessment and classroom learning. *Assessment in Education: Principles, Policy & Practice, 5*(1), 7–74.**: The foundational synthesis establishing the empirical case for formative assessment's large effect sizes. Phases II and III rely on this work for evidence about variance across studies and the premium of active-engagement practices. Essential starting point for any serious study of formative assessment.

- **Hattie, J., & Timperley, H. (2007). The power of feedback. *Review of Educational Research, 77*(1), 81–112.**: The most carefully theorized framework for understanding why different types of feedback produce different outcomes. The four-level taxonomy and the self-regulation-level premium are central to Phase III's evidence review. Essential reading for anyone designing feedback systems.

- **Nelson, T. O., & Narens, L. (1990). Metamemory: A theoretical framework and new findings. *Psychology of Learning and Motivation, 26*, 125–173.**: The foundational theoretical paper establishing the monitoring-control architecture that the MCM builds on. Phase IV's mechanism analysis is structurally organized around this framework. Required reading for understanding the cognitive architecture of calibration.

- **Kornell, N., & Bjork, R. A. (2009). A stability bias in human memory: Overestimating remembering and underestimating learning. *Journal of Experimental Psychology: General, 138*(4), 449–468.**: Direct evidence that retrieval practice produces calibration gains independent of retention gains — a key piece of evidence for the MCM's claim that the testing effect's mechanism includes a calibration component. Phase III draws on this finding.

- **Dunlosky, J., & Metcalfe, J. (2009). *Metacognition*. Sage.**: Comprehensive treatment of the metacognitive calibration literature. Provides the empirical foundation for the MCM's claims about monitoring accuracy and its role in learning. Essential reference for Phase IV's mechanism analysis.

- **Thiede, K. W., & Anderson, M. C. M. (2003). Summarizing can improve metacognitive accuracy. *Contemporary Educational Psychology, 28*(2), 129–160.**: Direct evidence that metacognitive accuracy can be improved through specific activities (delayed summarization) — evidence that monitoring accuracy is not fixed and that calibration events can be designed. Phase IV draws on this work.

- **Sadler, D. R. (1989). Formative assessment and the design of instructional systems. *Instructional Science, 18*(2), 119–144.**: The pre-Black & Wiliam foundational paper that first characterized formative assessment in terms of the gap between current and desired performance. Provides the intellectual ancestry for both the ITM and MCM, and is essential for understanding the historical development of the field.

- **Brown, A. L., & Palincsar, A. S. (1984). Reciprocal teaching of comprehension-fostering and comprehension-monitoring activities. *Cognition and Instruction, 1*(2), 117–175.**: Foundational study on comprehension monitoring training, demonstrating that monitoring accuracy can be trained and that trained monitoring produces learning gains. Phase III draws on this work as evidence that metacognitive calibration is a trainable component.

- **Bjork, R. A. (1994). Memory and metamemory considerations in the training of human beings. In J. Metcalfe & A. Shimamura (Eds.), *Metacognition: Knowing about knowing* (pp. 185–205). MIT Press.**: Foundational treatment of desirable difficulties as interventions that improve long-term learning by replacing fluency cues with retrieval cues. Directly supports the cue invalidity mechanism (Phase IV). Foundational for [[desirable-difficulties]] node.

- **Flavell, J. H. (1979). Metacognition and cognitive monitoring: A new area of cognitive-developmental inquiry. *American Psychologist*, 34(10), 906–911.**: The original framework paper introducing the metacognitive knowledge/experience/monitoring architecture. The source of the [[flavell-metacognition-framework]] node. Supports the three-component framework in Phase II.

- **Koriat, A. (1997). Monitoring one's own knowledge during study: A cue-utilization approach to judgments of learning. *Journal of Experimental Psychology: General*, 126(4), 349–370.**: Established the cue-utilization framework for metacognitive monitoring, demonstrating that JOLs are based on accessible cues rather than direct introspection. Core empirical support for the cue invalidity mechanism (Phase IV).

- **Kruger, J., & Dunning, D. (1999). Unskilled and unaware of it: How difficulties in recognizing one's own incompetence lead to inflated self-assessments. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*, 77(6), 1121–1134.**: The foundational Dunning-Kruger paper, documenting expertise-dependent monitoring accuracy. Supports the argument that monitoring accuracy is domain-specific and develops alongside domain knowledge (Phase III).

- **Nelson, T. O., & Narens, L. (1990). Metamemory: A theoretical framework and new findings. *Psychology of Learning and Motivation*, 26, 125–173.**: The meta-level/object-level model of metacognition. Defines the monitoring/control distinction foundational to the coupling analysis in Phase II. See [[Meta-Level-Object-Level-Model]].

- **Rawson, K. A., & Dunlosky, J. (2002). Are performance predictions for text based on ease of processing? *Journal of Experimental Psychology: Learning, Memory, and Cognition*, 28(1), 69–80.**: Empirical demonstration of the fluency-performance dissociation: disfluency lowers comprehension judgments while improving actual performance. Core evidence for the fluency illusion mechanism (Phase III).

- **Thiede, K. W., Anderson, M. C. M., & Therriault, D. (2003). Accuracy of metacognitive monitoring affects learning of texts. *Journal of Educational Psychology*, 95(1), 66–73.**: Documented the monitoring gap: students who accurately detected poor comprehension nonetheless defaulted to re-reading. The key empirical anchor for coupling failure distinct from monitoring failure (Phase III).

- **Winne, P. H., & Hadwin, A. F. (1998). Studying as self-regulated learning. In D. Hacker, J. Dunlosky, & A. Graesser (Eds.), *Metacognition in educational theory and practice* (pp. 277–304). Erlbaum.**: COPES model of self-regulated learning, identifying the cognitive processing steps between monitoring signals and regulatory action. Direct theoretical support for the threshold mechanism in Phase IV.

- **Zimmerman, B. J. (2000). Attaining self-regulation: A social cognitive perspective. In M. Boekaerts, P. Pintrich, & M. Zeidner (Eds.), *Handbook of self-regulation* (pp. 13–39). Academic Press.**: Zimmerman's three-phase SRL model. The analysis in Phase VI maps this model onto the infrastructure framework, deepening its theoretical account. See [[Zimmerman's-Three-Phase-SRL-Cycle]].

- **Brown, A. L. (1987). Metacognition, executive control, self-regulation, and other more mysterious mechanisms. In F. Weinert & R. Kluwe (Eds.), *Metacognition, motivation, and understanding* (pp. 65–116). Erlbaum.**: Extends Flavell's framework to regulation processes, distinguishing monitoring from control functions. Core theoretical support for the three-component architecture in Phase II.

- **Hacker, D. J., Dunlosky, J., & Graesser, A. C. (Eds.) (2009). *Handbook of Metacognition in Education*. Routledge.**: Comprehensive synthesis of empirical research on metacognition in educational contexts. The most authoritative reference for the monitoring-regulation relationship and its instructional implications. Supports Phases III and V broadly.

- **Perkins, D., & Salomon, G. (1992). Transfer of learning. *International Encyclopedia of Education*, 2(2), 6452–6457.**: Foundational treatment of near and far transfer, including the conditions under which structural insights from one domain travel to others. The theoretical grounding for the FAR transfer architecture in Phase VII.

- **Flavell, J. H. (1979). Metacognition and cognitive monitoring: A new area of cognitive-developmental inquiry. *American Psychologist, 34*(10), 906–911.**: The foundational paper introducing the metacognitive knowledge/metacognitive experience distinction that structures this report's Phase II framework. Establishes the two-component model that subsequent researchers, including Nelson and Narens, extend into process-level accounts. Supports Phases II and IV.

- **Nelson, T. O., & Narens, L. (1990). Metamemory: A theoretical framework and new findings. *Psychology of Learning and Motivation, 26*, 125–173.**: Introduces the monitoring-control hierarchical model that is the primary analytical tool of this report. The monitoring-control separation — with its implication that accurate monitoring does not automatically produce appropriate control — is the foundational insight on which this report's analysis of the monitoring-control dissociation in Phase III is built. Supports Phases II, IV, and V.

- **Wood, D., Bruner, J. S., & Ross, G. (1976). The role of tutoring in problem solving. *Journal of Child Psychology and Psychiatry, 17*(2), 89–100.**: Operational specification of the scaffolding construct with six tutoring functions. Introduces the contingency principle and the six functions of scaffolding that remain foundational to instructional design. Supports Phase II.

- **Bjork, R. A. (1994). Memory and metamemory considerations in the training of human beings. In J. Metcalfe & A. Shimamura (Eds.), *Metacognition: Knowing about knowing* (pp. 185–205). MIT Press.**: Foundational paper introducing the desirable difficulties framework. The argument that conditions improving long-term retention often slow initial acquisition is central to this report's Phase IV argument that effective metacognitive scaffolding works by making shallow processing harder rather than making learning easier. Supports Phases IV and V.

- **Koriat, A., & Bjork, R. A. (2005). Illusions of competence in monitoring one's knowledge during study. *Journal of Experimental Psychology: Learning, Memory, and Cognition, 31*(2), 187–194.**: Documents the fluency illusion and its systematic decoupling of felt understanding from actual learning. Central to this report's Phase IV mechanistic account of why unscaffolded monitoring is unreliable. Supports Phases III and IV.

- **Thiede, K. W., Anderson, M. C. M., & Therriault, D. (2003). Accuracy of metacognitive monitoring affects learning of texts. *Journal of Educational Psychology, 95*(1), 66–73.**: Documents the monitoring-without-control phenomenon: delayed summary generation improved monitoring accuracy for poorly understood texts but did not reliably produce increased restudy of those texts. Central evidence for the monitoring-control dissociation argument in Phase III. Supports Phase III.

- **Azevedo, R., & Cromley, J. G. (2004). Does training on self-regulated learning facilitate students' learning with hypermedia? *Journal of Educational Psychology, 96*(3), 523–535.**: Demonstrates adaptive scaffolding superiority over fixed scaffolding for complex learning tasks. Central evidence for the adaptiveness requirement in Phase III and the expertise reversal implications for Phase V. Supports Phases III and V.

- **Kalyuga, S., Chandler, P., Tuovinen, J., & Sweller, J. (2003). When problem solving is superior to studying worked examples. *Journal of Educational Psychology, 95*(2), 579–588.**: Documents the expertise reversal effect for instructional support. Important boundary condition for metacognitive scaffolding effectiveness — the support that benefits novices can harm experts. Supports Phases III and V.

- **Kapur, M. (2016). Examining productive failure, productive success, unproductive failure, and unproductive success in learning. *Educational Psychologist, 51*(2), 289–299.**: Systematic treatment of productive failure — conditions under which allowing learners to fail before instruction produces better transfer than instruction-first approaches. Directly relevant to the comfort trap in scaffolding design argument in Phase IV. Supports Phase IV.

- **Perkins, D. N., & Salomon, G. (1989). Are cognitive skills context-bound? *Educational Researcher, 18*(1), 16–25.**: Classic treatment of far transfer, arguing that metacognitive and thinking skills are among the most potentially transferable skills but that transfer is not spontaneous — it requires deliberate design conditions ("low road" and "high road" transfer). Relevant to the transfer-dependence failure mode in Phase V and the transfer encoding in Phase VII. Supports Phases V and VII.

- **Zimmerman, B. J. (2000). Attaining self-regulation: A social cognitive perspective. In M. Boekaerts, P. R. Pintrich, & M. Zeidner (Eds.), *Handbook of self-regulation* (pp. 13–39). Academic Press.**: Zimmerman's cyclical model of self-regulated learning provides the developmental endpoint that metacognitive scaffolding aims toward — the forethought, performance, and self-reflection cycle. Connects scaffolding design to the SRL framework throughout the report. Supports Phases II, V, and VI.

- **Kintsch, W. (1988). The role of knowledge in discourse comprehension: A construction-integration model. *Psychological Review, 95*(2), 163–182.**: The foundational paper establishing the Construction-Integration model of reading. Demonstrates that reading involves two phases — construction (activating a broad associative network) and integration (constraining to coherent representations) — and that the depth of Situation Model construction determines the quality of comprehension. Central to Phases II and IV.

- **Chi, M. T. H., de Leeuw, N., Chiu, M.-H., & LaVancher, C. (1994). Eliciting self-explanations improves understanding. *Cognitive Science, 18*(3), 439–477.**: The seminal demonstration of the Self-Explanation Effect. Shows that readers who explain texts to themselves during reading produce dramatically better comprehension and transfer than readers who read without self-explanation. The mechanism — forcing Constructive mode engagement — is central to this analysis's Phase III and IV arguments.

- **Stanovich, K. E. (1986). Matthew effects in reading: Some consequences of individual differences in the acquisition of literacy. *Reading Research Quarterly, 21*(4), 360–407.**: The foundational paper establishing the Matthew Effect in reading. Documents the compounding mechanism by which early reading advantages accumulate and proposes the vocabulary-working memory link central to this analysis's account of why critical analysis development is uneven across readers.

- **Baker, L., & Brown, A. L. (1984). Metacognitive skills and reading. In P. D. Pearson (Ed.), *Handbook of Reading Research* (pp. 353–394). Longman.**: The foundational treatment of comprehension monitoring in reading. Establishes that many readers fail to detect text inconsistencies and that comprehension monitoring can be taught. Directly supports this analysis's fourth mechanism and the Illusion of Knowing argument.

- **Pressley, M., & Afflerbach, P. (1995). *Verbal Protocols of Reading: The Nature of Constructively Responsive Reading.* Lawrence Erlbaum.**: A comprehensive treatment of think-aloud protocols during reading, demonstrating the constructive and evaluative operations that skilled readers perform during silent reading. Provides empirical grounding for the inference hierarchy proposed in Phase IV.

- **Craik, F. I. M., & Lockhart, R. S. (1972). Levels of processing: A framework for memory research. *Journal of Verbal Learning and Verbal Behavior, 11*(6), 671–684.**: The original levels-of-processing paper. Establishes that depth of semantic processing — not repetition or effort per se — determines retention and comprehension quality. Foundational to Phase II's framework and the distinction between elaborative and evaluative processing.

- **Chi, M. T. H., & Wylie, R. (2014). The ICAP framework: Linking cognitive engagement to active learning outcomes. *Educational Psychologist, 49*(4), 219–243.**: The paper establishing the ICAP Framework used as a central analytical tool in Phase II. Demonstrates that Constructive and Interactive engagement modes produce significantly better learning outcomes than Active and Passive modes, and that the mode of engagement is not determined by the activity but by the cognitive operations the learner performs.

- **Bjork, R. A. (1994). Memory and metamemory considerations in the training of human beings. In J. Metcalfe & A. Shimamura (Eds.), *Metacognition: Knowing about Knowing* (pp. 185–205). MIT Press.**: The foundational treatment of Desirable Difficulties. Establishes that processing conditions that feel more effortful produce better retention and transfer, and that fluency is a misleading guide to learning depth. Connected to the Fluency Illusion argument in Phase III.

- **Rayner, K. (1998). Eye movements in reading and information processing: 20 years of research. *Psychological Bulletin, 124*(3), 372–422.**: The most comprehensive review of eye-tracking data on reading behavior, documenting regression patterns and their relationship to inference generation and complex text processing. Provides empirical grounding for the self-pacing mechanism in Phase III.

- **Baron, J. (1995). Myside bias in thinking about abortion. *Thinking and Reasoning, 1*(3), 221–235.**: An important empirical paper on actively open-minded thinking and myside bias. Documents the tendency for motivated reasoning to filter evidence in self-serving ways, directly relevant to Phase V's argument about the confirmation bias ceiling of solo silent reading and the necessity of social discourse.

- **Baddeley, A. D., & Hitch, G. (1974). Working memory. In G. H. Bower (Ed.), *The Psychology of Learning and Motivation, Vol. 8* (pp. 47–89). Academic Press.**: The foundational working memory model paper, establishing the distinction between the phonological loop, visuospatial sketchpad, and central executive. The phonological loop mechanism central to this analysis is rooted in this framework.

- **Rayner, K., Schotter, E. R., Masson, M. E. J., Potter, M. C., & Treiman, R. (2016). So Much to Read, So Little Time: How Do We Read, and Can Speed Reading Help? *Psychological Science in the Public Interest, 17*(1), 4–34.**: The most comprehensive scientific review of speed-reading claims against the eye movement evidence base. Systematically examines RSVP, peripheral vision claims, and sub-vocalization suppression. Supports Phases II and III. Essential reading for anyone evaluating commercial speed-reading products.

- **Perfetti, C. A. (2007). Reading Ability: Lexical Quality to Comprehension. *Scientific Studies of Reading, 11*(4), 357–383.**: Presents the Lexical Quality Hypothesis — the theoretical foundation for the vocabulary automaticity argument in Phase IV. Argues that the quality of lexical representations (their precision, completeness, and consolidation) is the primary determinant of reading ability differences. Strongly supports the highest-leverage intervention recommendation.

- **Kintsch, W., & van Dijk, T. A. (1978). Toward a Model of Text Comprehension and Production. *Psychological Review, 85*(5), 363–394.**: Original articulation of the distinction between propositional text base and situation model, foundational for Phase II's definition of comprehension. Provides the theoretical grounding for why surface-level reading metrics are insufficient as comprehension standards.

- **Carver, R. P. (1990). *Reading Rate: A Review of Research and Theory*. Academic Press.**: Comprehensive review of reading rate research establishing the empirical base rate of skilled adult reading speed. Introduces the concept of "rauding" (efficient reading at the optimal rate for comprehension), providing the theoretical foundation for the comprehension-indexed speed metric. Supports Phase III.

- **Rayner, K. (1998). Eye Movements in Reading and Information Processing: 20 Years of Research. *Psychological Bulletin, 124*(3), 372–422.**: The definitive summary of two decades of eye movement research in reading, establishing the perceptual span constraint and fixation duration norms. Foundational for Phase III's evidence review on the mechanics of skilled reading.

- **Baddeley, A. D. (2000). The Episodic Buffer: A New Component of Working Memory? *Trends in Cognitive Sciences, 4*(11), 417–423.**: Updated working memory model including the episodic buffer, directly relevant to the situation model construction bottleneck. Provides theoretical grounding for Phase IV's treatment of working memory integration demands during reading.

- **Ausubel, D. P. (1960). The Use of Advance Organizers in the Learning and Retention of Meaningful Verbal Material. *Journal of Educational Psychology, 51*(5), 267–272.**: Original advance organizer study, providing empirical grounding for the prior knowledge pre-activation strategy in Phase V. Demonstrably improves both comprehension and retention through schema pre-activation.

- **Ericsson, K. A., & Kintsch, W. (1995). Long-Term Working Memory. *Psychological Review, 102*(2), 211–245.**: Theoretical account of how experts use long-term memory structures to extend effective working memory capacity in their domain. Provides the mechanistic bridge between domain expertise and the reading speed advantage — supports the schema-as-inference-engine analysis in Phase IV.

- **Bjork, R. A., & Bjork, E. L. (2011). Making Things Hard on Yourself, but in a Good Way: Creating Desirable Difficulties to Enhance Learning. In M. A. Gernsbacher et al. (Eds.), *Psychology and the Real World*. Worth Publishers.**: Foundational for the [[fluency-illusion]] analysis — demonstrates that subjective ease of processing is negatively correlated with actual learning depth. Directly relevant to the speed-monitoring incompatibility and the risks of bandwidth compression training.

- **Graesser, A. C., Singer, M., & Trabasso, T. (1994). Constructing Inferences During Narrative Text Comprehension. *Psychological Review, 101*(3), 371–395.**: Comprehensive framework for the types of inferences generated during reading and their resource demands. Provides the inferential bridging analysis underpinning Bottleneck 3 in Phase II, and grounds the prior knowledge pre-activation argument of Phase V.

- **Baker, L., & Brown, A. L. (1984). Metacognitive skills and reading. In P. D. Pearson (Ed.), *Handbook of Reading Research*. New York: Longman.**: Foundational treatment of comprehension monitoring and fix-up strategies during reading. Directly supports the analysis in Section III of monitoring mechanisms and their failure modes.

- **Bjork, R. A., Dunlosky, J., & Kornell, N. (2013). Self-regulated learning: Beliefs, techniques, and illusions. *Annual Review of Psychology*, 64, 417-444.**: Comprehensive review of metacognitive illusions — fluency illusion, overconfidence, desirable difficulties — that informed the calibration analysis in Section V. Key source for the argument that subjective learning signals are systematically misleading.

- **Brown, A. L. (1987). Metacognition, executive control, self-regulation, and other more mysterious mechanisms. In F. E. Weinert & R. H. Kluwe (Eds.), *Metacognition, Motivation, and Understanding* (pp. 65-116). Hillsdale, NJ: Lawrence Erlbaum.**: Established the knowledge-of-cognition vs. regulation-of-cognition distinction that structures the entire report. Essential reading for understanding the dual nature of metacognition.

- **Clark, A., & Chalmers, D. J. (1998). The extended mind. *Analysis*, 58(1), 7-19.**: The foundational paper for the extended mind thesis, providing the philosophical framework for the Extended Metacognitive Architecture Model (EMAM) developed in Section VI. The parity principle is the key argumentative tool.

- **Flavell, J. H. (1979). Metacognition and cognitive monitoring: A new area of cognitive-developmental inquiry. *American Psychologist*, 34(10), 906-911.**: The seminal paper introducing metacognition as a research programme. Establishes the taxonomy of metacognitive knowledge (person, task, strategy) and introduces the concept of metacognitive experiences. Starting point for Section I.

- **Kruger, J., & Dunning, D. (1999). Unskilled and unaware of it: How difficulties in recognizing one's own incompetence lead to inflated self-assessments. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*, 77(6), 1121-1134.**: Original paper on the Dunning-Kruger effect. Supports the calibration analysis in Section V regarding the structural bootstrapping problem of metacognitive assessment.

- **Nelson, T. O., & Narens, L. (1990). Metamemory: A theoretical framework and new findings. In G. H. Bower (Ed.), *The Psychology of Learning and Motivation* (Vol. 26, pp. 125-173). New York: Academic Press.**: The paper that established the meta-level/object-level model of metacognitive monitoring and control. Foundational for the monitoring-control architecture analysed throughout the report.

- **Roediger, H. L., & Karpicke, J. D. (2006). Test-enhanced learning: Taking memory tests improves long-term retention. *Psychological Science*, 17(3), 249-255.**: Influential demonstration of the testing effect. Supports the argument in Sections III and V that retrieval practice provides more accurate metacognitive monitoring signals than re-reading.

- **Schraw, G. (1998). Promoting general metacognitive awareness. *Instructional Science*, 26, 113-125.**: Synthesised the tripartite model of metacognitive knowledge (declarative, procedural, conditional) used in Section I. The conditional knowledge component is particularly important for the report's analysis of strategy selection in PKM contexts.

- **Sparrow, B., Liu, J., & Wegner, D. M. (2011). Google effects on memory: Cognitive consequences of having information at our fingertips. *Science*, 333(6043), 776-778.**: Empirical evidence for the cognitive offloading effect discussed in Section V. Demonstrates that knowledge of external storage availability reduces internal encoding effort, supporting the PKM calibration paradox analysis.

- **Zimmerman, B. J. (2000). Attaining self-regulation: A social cognitive perspective. In M. Boekaerts, P. R. Pintrich, & M. Zeidner (Eds.), *Handbook of Self-Regulation* (pp. 13-39). San Diego: Academic Press.**: The foundational articulation of the three-phase SRL cycle (forethought → performance → self-reflection) that provides the structural framework for the PKM-SRL isomorphism analysed in Section IV.

- **Pintrich, P. R. (2000). The role of goal orientation in self-regulated learning. In M. Boekaerts, P. R. Pintrich, & M. Zeidner (Eds.), *Handbook of Self-Regulation* (pp. 451-502). San Diego: Academic Press.**: Develops the four-column SRL framework integrating cognitive, motivational, behavioural, and contextual regulation. Supports the report's acknowledgment of motivational dimensions discussed in Section IV and the limitations section.

- **Flavell, J. H. (1979). Metacognition and cognitive monitoring: A new area of cognitive-developmental inquiry. *American Psychologist*, 34(10), 906–911. https://doi.org/10.1037/0003-066X.34.10.906**: The foundational paper for the entire field. Flavell introduces the term "metacognition," provides the tripartite taxonomy of metacognitive knowledge, experiences, and regulation, and outlines the research program that would follow. Essential reading for understanding the conceptual origins of all metacognitive reading research.

**Recommended Sections:** Full text; particularly pp. 906–909 for the taxonomy.

- **Baker, L., & Brown, A. L. (1984). Metacognitive skills and reading. In P. D. Pearson (Ed.), *Handbook of reading research* (pp. 353–394). Longman.**: The defining synthesis of metacognitive principles applied to reading comprehension. Baker and Brown articulate comprehension monitoring, its evaluation standards, and the fix-up strategy repertoire with a precision that subsequent research has not substantially superseded. Essential for Phases III and IV of this report.

**Recommended Sections:** Sections on evaluation standards (pp. 359–368) and regulatory responses (pp. 368–382).

- **Nelson, T. O., & Narens, L. (1990). Metamemory: A theoretical framework and new findings. *Psychology of Learning and Motivation*, 26, 125–173. https://doi.org/10.1016/S0079-7421(08)60053-5**: Establishes the object-level/meta-level architecture and the monitoring-control framework that provides the formal computational backbone for the field. Indispensable for the mechanistic analysis in Phase IV; the monitoring-control coupling concept derives directly from this framework.

**Recommended Sections:** Sections 2 and 3 on the formal framework; the empirical work on JoL and FoK.

- **Palincsar, A. S., & Brown, A. L. (1984). Reciprocal teaching of comprehension-fostering and comprehension-monitoring activities. *Cognition and Instruction*, 1(2), 117–175. https://doi.org/10.1207/s1532690xci0102_1**: The original reciprocal teaching paper. Documents the development, theoretical rationale, and initial empirical evaluation of the intervention. Required reading for Section 5.1 of this report; provides the most important single example of metacognitive reading theory instantiated in pedagogical practice.

**Recommended Sections:** The theoretical rationale section (pp. 117–124); the transcript examples throughout.

- **Schraw, G., & Moshman, D. (1995). Metacognitive theories. *Educational Psychology Review*, 7(4), 351–371. https://doi.org/10.1007/BF02212307**: Provides the bipartite knowledge-regulation taxonomy and the Metacognitive Awareness Inventory. The most widely cited single account of metacognitive structure in educational psychology.

- **Kintsch, W. (1988). The role of knowledge in discourse comprehension: A construction-integration model. *Psychological Review*, 95(2), 163–182. https://doi.org/10.1037/0033-295X.95.2.163**: Establishes the Construction-Integration model of discourse comprehension, including the textbase/situation model distinction that anchors the conceptual framework in Phase II.

- **Pressley, M., & Afflerbach, P. (1995). *Verbal protocols of reading: The nature of constructively responsive reading*. Erlbaum.**: The most comprehensive empirical account of skilled reading using think-aloud protocols; documents the metacognitive strategies that expert readers actually deploy, as distinct from what they report deploying. Essential for Section 4 and for understanding the gap between self-report and behavioral evidence.

- **Gough, P. B., & Tunmer, W. E. (1986). Decoding, reading, and reading disability. *Remedial and Special Education*, 7(1), 6–10. https://doi.org/10.1177/074193258600700104**: Original paper establishing the Simple View of Reading; the foundation for Section 2.2's account of the decoding-comprehension architecture.

- **Zimmerman, B. J. (2000). Attaining self-regulation: A social-cognitive perspective. In M. Boekaerts, P. R. Pintrich, & M. Zeidner (Eds.), *Handbook of self-regulation* (pp. 13–39). Academic Press.**: Zimmerman's systematization of his cyclic SRL model; the theoretical source for Phase III.4's treatment of self-regulated reading within the SRL framework.

- **Paris, S. G., Lipson, M. Y., & Wixson, K. K. (1983). Becoming a strategic reader. *Contemporary Educational Psychology*, 8(3), 293–316. https://doi.org/10.1016/0361-476X(83)90018-8**: Introduces the declarative/procedural/conditional trichotomy in the reading strategy context; the theoretical foundation for Phase III.3's treatment of the Schraw-Moshman framework.

- **Perfetti, C. A. (1985). *Reading ability*. Oxford University Press.**: The foundational text for Verbal Efficiency Theory; establishes the decoding-efficiency mechanism by which fluency constrains comprehension resources.

- **van Dijk, T. A., & Kintsch, W. (1983). *Strategies of discourse comprehension*. Academic Press.**: Original source for the situation model construct; provides the full three-level text representation architecture (surface code, textbase, situation model).

- **Duffy, G. G., Roehler, L. R., Sivan, E., Rackliffe, G., Book, C., Meloth, M. S., ... & Bassiri, D. (1987). Effects of explaining the reasoning associated with using reading strategies. *Reading Research Quarterly*, 22(3), 347–368.**: Empirical evaluation of explicit reasoning instruction for reading strategies; demonstrates that explaining *why* strategies work (conditional knowledge) is critical for transfer — a key finding for Section 5.2.

- **Rosenshine, B., & Meister, C. (1994). Reciprocal teaching: A review of the research. *Review of Educational Research*, 64(4), 479–530. https://doi.org/10.3102/00346543064004479**: Comprehensive meta-analysis of reciprocal teaching research; the primary empirical source for Section 5.1's efficacy claims.

- **Flavell, J. H. (1979). Metacognition and cognitive monitoring: A new area of cognitive-developmental inquiry. *American Psychologist*, 34(10), 906–911. https://doi.org/10.1037/0003-066X.34.10.906**: The foundational paper for the entire field. Flavell introduces the term "metacognition," provides the tripartite taxonomy of metacognitive knowledge, experiences, and regulation, and outlines the research program that would follow. Essential reading for understanding the conceptual origins of all metacognitive reading research.

**Recommended Sections:** Full text; particularly pp. 906–909 for the taxonomy.

- **Baker, L., & Brown, A. L. (1984). Metacognitive skills and reading. In P. D. Pearson (Ed.), *Handbook of reading research* (pp. 353–394). Longman.**: The defining synthesis of metacognitive principles applied to reading comprehension. Baker and Brown articulate comprehension monitoring, its evaluation standards, and the fix-up strategy repertoire with a precision that subsequent research has not substantially superseded. Essential for Phases III and IV of this report.

**Recommended Sections:** Sections on evaluation standards (pp. 359–368) and regulatory responses (pp. 368–382).

- **Nelson, T. O., & Narens, L. (1990). Metamemory: A theoretical framework and new findings. *Psychology of Learning and Motivation*, 26, 125–173. https://doi.org/10.1016/S0079-7421(08)60053-5**: Establishes the object-level/meta-level architecture and the monitoring-control framework that provides the formal computational backbone for the field. Indispensable for the mechanistic analysis in Phase IV; the monitoring-control coupling concept derives directly from this framework.

**Recommended Sections:** Sections 2 and 3 on the formal framework; the empirical work on JoL and FoK.

- **Palincsar, A. S., & Brown, A. L. (1984). Reciprocal teaching of comprehension-fostering and comprehension-monitoring activities. *Cognition and Instruction*, 1(2), 117–175. https://doi.org/10.1207/s1532690xci0102_1**: The original reciprocal teaching paper. Documents the development, theoretical rationale, and initial empirical evaluation of the intervention. Required reading for Section 5.1 of this report; provides the most important single example of metacognitive reading theory instantiated in pedagogical practice.

**Recommended Sections:** The theoretical rationale section (pp. 117–124); the transcript examples throughout.

- **Schraw, G., & Moshman, D. (1995). Metacognitive theories. *Educational Psychology Review*, 7(4), 351–371. https://doi.org/10.1007/BF02212307**: Provides the bipartite knowledge-regulation taxonomy and the Metacognitive Awareness Inventory. The most widely cited single account of metacognitive structure in educational psychology.

- **Kintsch, W. (1988). The role of knowledge in discourse comprehension: A construction-integration model. *Psychological Review*, 95(2), 163–182. https://doi.org/10.1037/0033-295X.95.2.163**: Establishes the Construction-Integration model of discourse comprehension, including the textbase/situation model distinction that anchors the conceptual framework in Phase II.

- **Pressley, M., & Afflerbach, P. (1995). *Verbal protocols of reading: The nature of constructively responsive reading*. Erlbaum.**: The most comprehensive empirical account of skilled reading using think-aloud protocols; documents the metacognitive strategies that expert readers actually deploy, as distinct from what they report deploying. Essential for Section 4 and for understanding the gap between self-report and behavioral evidence.

- **Gough, P. B., & Tunmer, W. E. (1986). Decoding, reading, and reading disability. *Remedial and Special Education*, 7(1), 6–10. https://doi.org/10.1177/074193258600700104**: Original paper establishing the Simple View of Reading; the foundation for Section 2.2's account of the decoding-comprehension architecture.

- **Zimmerman, B. J. (2000). Attaining self-regulation: A social-cognitive perspective. In M. Boekaerts, P. R. Pintrich, & M. Zeidner (Eds.), *Handbook of self-regulation* (pp. 13–39). Academic Press.**: Zimmerman's systematization of his cyclic SRL model; the theoretical source for Phase III.4's treatment of self-regulated reading within the SRL framework.

- **Paris, S. G., Lipson, M. Y., & Wixson, K. K. (1983). Becoming a strategic reader. *Contemporary Educational Psychology*, 8(3), 293–316. https://doi.org/10.1016/0361-476X(83)90018-8**: Introduces the declarative/procedural/conditional trichotomy in the reading strategy context; the theoretical foundation for Phase III.3's treatment of the Schraw-Moshman framework.

- **Perfetti, C. A. (1985). *Reading ability*. Oxford University Press.**: The foundational text for Verbal Efficiency Theory; establishes the decoding-efficiency mechanism by which fluency constrains comprehension resources.

- **van Dijk, T. A., & Kintsch, W. (1983). *Strategies of discourse comprehension*. Academic Press.**: Original source for the situation model construct; provides the full three-level text representation architecture (surface code, textbase, situation model).

- **Duffy, G. G., Roehler, L. R., Sivan, E., Rackliffe, G., Book, C., Meloth, M. S., ... & Bassiri, D. (1987). Effects of explaining the reasoning associated with using reading strategies. *Reading Research Quarterly*, 22(3), 347–368.**: Empirical evaluation of explicit reasoning instruction for reading strategies; demonstrates that explaining *why* strategies work (conditional knowledge) is critical for transfer — a key finding for Section 5.2.

- **Rosenshine, B., & Meister, C. (1994). Reciprocal teaching: A review of the research. *Review of Educational Research*, 64(4), 479–530. https://doi.org/10.3102/00346543064004479**: Comprehensive meta-analysis of reciprocal teaching research; the primary empirical source for Section 5.1's efficacy claims.

- **Flavell, J. H. (1979). Metacognition and cognitive monitoring: A new area of cognitive-developmental inquiry. *American Psychologist*, 34(10), 906–911.**: The foundational paper that introduced metacognition as a formal research construct. Flavell proposed the tripartite distinction between metacognitive knowledge, experiences, and regulation that has organized the field ever since. Essential reading for any serious engagement with metacognitive theory; remarkably accessible given its foundational status.

- **Zimmerman, B. J. (2000). Attaining self-regulation: A social cognitive perspective. In M. Boekaerts, P. R. Pintrich, & M. Zeidner (Eds.), *Handbook of Self-Regulation* (pp. 13–39). Academic Press.**: Zimmerman's comprehensive statement of the cyclical SRL model, synthesizing decades of empirical research. Covers the forethought-performance-reflection cycle in depth and connects metacognitive regulation to motivational and volitional processes. The most comprehensive single source for Zimmerman's framework.

- **Bjork, R. A., & Bjork, E. L. (2011). Making things hard on yourself, but in a good way: Creating desirable difficulties to enhance learning. In M. A. Gernsbacher, R. W. Pew, L. M. Hough, & J. R. Pomerantz (Eds.), *Psychology and the Real World* (pp. 59–68). Worth Publishers.**: An accessible synthesis of the desirable difficulties research program, including evidence for spaced practice, interleaving, and retrieval practice. Directly addresses the gap between subjective learning experience and objective learning outcomes — the core practical problem of metacognitive calibration.

- **Roediger, H. L., & Karpicke, J. D. (2006). Test-enhanced learning: Taking memory tests improves long-term retention. *Psychological Science*, 17(3), 249–255.**: The landmark study establishing the testing effect under ecologically valid conditions. Participants who studied through retrieval practice retained 50% more material one week later than those who restudied. This paper is foundational for any practitioner interested in applying metacognitive research to actual study habits.

- **Kruger, J., & Dunning, D. (1999).
